Method: RouteRepair diagnoses parent-specific weaknesses from instance-level performance, applies targeted modifications to corresponding heuristic components while protecting well-performing behavior, combines routing evidence, solver behavior, and program context to define bounded repair objectives, and validates each intervention through matched parent-child evaluation.

Result: RouteRepair-GLS reduces TSP optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587%, constructive CVRP heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to savings heuristic, and generated ACO priors outperform hand-designed priors.

Conclusion: Failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can effectively improve routing heuristics on difficult instances while preserving performance on cases they already solve well.

Abstract: Efficient routing optimization is essential to freight transportation, urban logistics, and shared mobility, where high-quality heuristics are often required under limited computational budgets. Recent large language model (LLM)-based automated heuristic design methods can generate effective routing rules, but aggregate evaluation may mask recurrent failures on particular instance structures. To address this limitation, this study develops RouteRepair, which diagnoses parent-specific weaknesses from instance-level performance and applies targeted modifications to the corresponding heuristic components while protecting behavior that already performs well. Routing evidence, solver behavior, and program context are combined to define bounded repair objectives, and each intervention is validated through matched parent-child evaluation of failure recovery and collateral degradation. Experiments on the traveling salesman problem (TSP) and capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) span constructive search, guided local search, and ant colony optimization. RouteRepair-GLS reduces the mean TSP optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587%, while the constructive CVRP heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to the savings heuristic; the generated ACO priors also outperform matched hand-designed priors. These results show that failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can improve routing heuristics on difficult instances while preserving performance on cases they already solve well.

</details>


### [51] [Flexible and Interpretable Accent Distance Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11458)
*Charles McGhee,Mark J. F. Gales,Kate M. Knill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Articulatory representations from articulatory inversion provide an interpretable basis for accent comparison, with optimal transport enabling comparison across arbitrary recording types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for measuring accent differences have limitations - phonetic approaches using vowel formants are interpretable but require time-consuming word recordings that may not represent connected speech, while TTS research uses accent embeddings that are not interpretable despite being producible from any speech recording.

Method: Uses articulatory representations created through articulatory inversion as an interpretable basis for accent comparison, combined with optimal transport framework to enable accent comparison across different types of speech recordings.

Result: The proposed approach demonstrates that articulatory representations can serve as interpretable features for accent comparison, and optimal transport provides a mathematical framework for comparing accents regardless of the recording type or speech context.

Conclusion: Articulatory inversion provides an interpretable alternative to existing accent comparison methods, offering both interpretability and flexibility when combined with optimal transport for cross-recording comparison.

Abstract: Determining the differences between two speakers' accents is a fundamental task in linguistics and speech technology research. The methodology used to measure these differences depends on the specific research area. A phonetics researcher may demonstrate accent variation by comparing vowel formants in paired recordings of individual words. These results will be interpretable, but the recordings will be time-consuming to collect and may not be representative of connected speech. Accented Text-to-Speech (TTS) research has pushed towards using accent embeddings derived from accent classification tasks. These embeddings can be produced from any speech recording, but are not readily interpretable. In this paper, we demonstrate that articulatory representations created through articulatory inversion can be used as an interpretable basis for accent comparison and that optimal transport provides a framework for accent comparison across arbitrary recording types.

</details>


### [52] [The Convention Gap: Towards Measuring Implicit Communication in Cooperative AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11489)
*Makoto Fukushima,Hua-Dong Xiong,Ehsan Moradi Pari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The convention gap metric measures implicit communication in cooperative AI by comparing literal information-based failure predictions against actual failure rates, revealing significant differences between human-human, AI-AI, and human-AI interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-AI benchmarks may not capture human cooperative conventions—implicit protocols for reading meaning beyond literal messages—which are crucial for effective human-AI collaboration.

Method: Proposed the "convention gap" metric and applied it to analyze 101,000 play actions from three datasets (human-human, AI-AI, human-AI) in the Hanabi card game where the posterior is exactly computable due to deterministic hint constraints.

Result: Human pairs showed +26.2pp convention gap, AI pairs -0.7pp, and human-AI pairs +16.4pp. The gap was concentrated on plays with no hints (+46pp in human pairs). In human-AI play, gap varied by AI partner (6.2-24.1pp), with higher gap correlating with fewer human failures. Controlled experiments with Off-Belief Learning agents showed gap rising from +1.6pp to +21.7pp as convention content increased.

Conclusion: Convention compatibility better predicts an AI's effectiveness with human partners than AI-AI performance metrics, suggesting that measuring implicit communication through the convention gap can guide development of more human-compatible cooperative AI.

Abstract: Cooperative AI agents are evaluated against other AIs, yet human cooperation relies on implicit conventions---shared protocols for reading meaning beyond the literal message---which AI-AI benchmarks may not capture. We propose the \emph{convention gap}, the difference between the failure probability predicted from the literal content of communication and the observed failure rate, as a metric of implicit communication. In the card game Hanabi, the finite deck and deterministic hint constraints make this posterior exactly computable. We replayed about 101,000 play actions from three public datasets of human-human (hanab.live), AI-AI (HOAD), and human-AI (HanabiData) games. The gap was +26.2 percentage points (pp) in human pairs, $-$0.7~pp in AI pairs, and +16.4~pp in human-AI pairs, and was concentrated on plays of cards that had received no hints (+46~pp in human pairs). Within human-AI play, the literal information available to humans was similar across the three AI partners (mean predicted failure 38--41\%), but human failure rates ranged from 14.4\% to 34.4\% and the gap from +24.1 to +6.2~pp; the partner eliciting the largest gap produced the fewest human failures. Game score carried different information: it depended on each corpus's roster composition, whereas the gap separated human from AI play at the agent level. As a known-answer check, Off-Belief Learning agents, whose convention content is controlled by construction, gave a gap of +1.6~pp at the convention-free level, rising monotonically to +21.7~pp. These results suggest that convention compatibility, rather than AI-AI performance, may predict an AI's effectiveness with human partners.

</details>


### [53] [Published Unlearning Numbers Move Per Checkpoint, and Not Because the Removed Data Survives: An Audit of 263 Released Batch-Normalized Checkpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11490)
*Junlong Shen Xingyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Unlearning audits can be compromised when models ship batch-normalization statistics that weren't properly recorded or fitted, allowing checkpoints to drift and produce different verdicts than properly refitted models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Models released with unlearning claims often include batch-normalization statistics that weren't properly documented or fitted through gradient steps, creating a potential channel for audit inaccuracies when these statistics aren't properly accounted for.

Method: Refitting batch-normalization statistics on kept data at bit-identical weights to identify checkpoints that drift from their original release state, comparing how checkpoints move relative to retrained references, and analyzing whether data survival or state drift causes the movement.

Result: 47 of 221 released checkpoints moved past the expected variation from their own release seeds when properly refitted; checkpoint properties (not methods) moved; state drift (not data survival) caused movement; 12 verdicts crossed, 4 cleared recalibration budgets, 2 cleared on every replicate.

Conclusion: Releases should explicitly name the fitting convention alongside performance numbers, particularly for batch-normalized vision models where this audit vulnerability exists, to ensure proper unlearning verification.

Abstract: An unlearning audit reads its verdict off numbers that an unlearned model and its retrained reference each publish, and both also ship batch-normalization statistics that no gradient step wrote and no release records. Refitting them on kept data at bit-identical weights moves 47 of 221 released checkpoints past the spread their own release's seeds show, several inside a method whose average does not move: what moves is the checkpoint's property, not its method's. What does the moving is not the removed data surviving in the state: exchanging kept records for removed ones inside a fixed fitting pool moves a published cell by almost nothing, while how far a checkpoint's shipped state has drifted from any refit does track it. The consequence for a published decision is real but narrow: twelve verdicts cross, four clear a measured recalibration budget, two clear it on every replicate, and a population we trained and sited near its own criterion yields none. A release should therefore name the fitting convention beside the number, on the batch-normalized vision models where this channel exists.

</details>


### [54] [From Document Silos to Process Intelligence: A Multi-Layer Knowledge Graph for CMC Process Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11493)
*Reza Amirmoshiri,Faryad Sahneh,Yasser Jangjou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A modular agentic-AI platform transforms heterogeneous CMC process development documents into a queryable dual-layer knowledge graph (lexical + intelligence layers) with LLM agents routing queries, achieving 95% multiple-choice accuracy on proprietary pharma data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CMC process development generates enormous, fragmented technical information across functions and heterogeneous formats, causing traceability gaps and high knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing in pharmaceutical development.

Method: Developed a modular agentic-AI platform with dual-layer knowledge graph: 1) Base lexical layer with Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy for lossless ingestion of digital/scanned/handwritten/multilingual documents, and 2) Intelligence layer extracting ontology-aligned entities and bridging cross-document concepts via provenance-anchored domain graph. LLM agents operate across both layers with question-type routing.

Result: Evaluated on 505 questions from 38 development reports of Sanofi small-molecule program: Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy 95%, Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate 85% (degrades on comparative/corpus-wide questions revealing failure taxonomy). Router agent successfully selects between layers based on question type.

Conclusion: The protocol enables assessment of agentic platforms on nonpublic databases, and graph-based architectures will see broader pharma adoption for transforming fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence, addressing significant knowledge-management challenges in CMC development.

Abstract: Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls (CMC) process development generates an enormous body of technical information across a multi-stage, knowledge-intensive continuum from drug discovery to commercial manufacturing. This knowledge is traditionally fragmented across functions and heterogeneous formats, causing traceability gaps and significant knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing. We present a modular agentic-AI platform that converts a heterogeneous corpus of process-development documents into a queryable, dual-layer knowledge graph. A base knowledge layer builds a lexical graph with a Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy through lossless ingestion of digital, scanned, handwritten, and multilingual documents, while an intelligence layer extracts ontology-aligned entities and bridges cross-document concepts through a provenance-anchored domain graph. LLM agents operate across both layers, selecting the retrieval path best suited to each question. We evaluate the lexical layer with a novel three-tier protocol measuring the deployment-fidelity of a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system on proprietary data, demonstrated on 505 questions curated from 38 development reports of a Sanofi small-molecule program. Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy of 95% signals strong platform reliability; the stricter Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate of 85%, which degrades on comparative and corpus-wide questions, reveals a failure taxonomy that Tier-1 accuracy alone fails to capture. A router agent selects between layers according to question type. We anticipate this protocol will enable future designers of agentic platforms to assess their systems against nonpublic databases, and that graph-based architectures will see broader adoption in pharma as a means of transforming fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence.

</details>


### [55] [ActMap: Single-Pass Uncertainty Quantification from Generation-Time Activation Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11498)
*Jacopo Dardini,Roberta Calegari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ActMap: White-box UQ method compresses generation-time hidden states into compact tensor for trustworthiness scoring without multiple sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UQ methods either require multiple generations (inefficient), use only output-token probabilities (limited), or reduce internal computation to single hidden states (lossy). Need practical method for single-generation trust decisions.

Method: Compresses hidden-state trajectory (every layer, every generated token) into fixed 12×32×128 tensor of temporal-statistic channels. Captured during generation with no overhead, fixed shape across models. Lightweight classifier (compact Vision Transformer or MLP) reads correctness probability from map.

Result: Outperforms sampling, token-probability, attention, and embedding baselines on QA, math, and summarization tasks with 7-8B models. Matches performance of detector trained on 67× larger activation tensors with lower calibration error in most cases.

Conclusion: ActMap enables practical uncertainty quantification from single generation, supporting abstention, routing, and selective verification for scalable oversight of deployed models.

Abstract: Practical uncertainty quantification (UQ) for large language models must decide,
  from a single generation, whether a specific answer should be trusted. Existing
  methods either sample multiple generations, read only output-token probabilities,
  or reduce the model's internal computation to a single hidden state. We introduce
  ActMap, a white-box representation that compresses the generation-time hidden-
  state trajectory (every layer, every generated token) into a fixed $12 \times 32
  \times 128$ tensor of temporal-statistic channels that preserves structure across
  transformer depth and pooled hidden coordinates. The map is captured during the
  generation pass with no measurable overhead, has a fixed shape across model
  depths and hidden sizes, and occupies 96 KiB: a compact artifact that can be
  retained for audit-relevant generations and probed directly, with occlusion
  analysis localizing the classifier's signal to mid-depth regions of the map. A
  lightweight classifier, instantiated as a compact Vision Transformer, reads an
  estimated correctness probability from each map in a fraction of a millisecond;
  capacity-matched MLPs perform comparably, indicating the representation itself
  carries the result. Trained and evaluated in-domain on short-answer QA, direct-
  answer math, and summarization factuality with three instruction-tuned 7-8B
  models, ActMap consistently outperforms sampling, token-probability, attention,
  and embedding baselines, and matches ACT-ViT, a detector trained on dense
  activation tensors $67 \times$ larger, at essentially the same mean AUROC with
  lower calibration error on ten of twelve pairs. The resulting score supports
  abstention, routing, and selective verification from a single generation, making
  it a practical primitive for scalable oversight of deployed models.

</details>


### [56] [Extending SMT Solving with Non-Ground Clause Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11509)
*Yasmine Briefs,Christoph Weidenbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new calculus combining ground instantiation, CDCL(T)-style reasoning, and non-ground conflict analysis for SMT solving that learns more general clauses than ground conflicts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SMT solvers use quantifier instantiation but only learn ground clauses from conflicts, even though conflicts come from non-ground clauses. Non-ground reasoning can give exponentially shorter proofs than purely ground reasoning.

Method: Proposes a calculus with: 1) ground instantiations, 2) CDCL(T)-style rules, and 3) non-ground conflict analysis. Solver reasons on ground instances but performs resolution steps of conflict analysis on original non-ground clauses, producing more general learned clauses.

Result: The approach can learn non-redundant clauses with suitable strategy and includes chronological backtracking. The calculus provides a common framework that simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and Resolution.

Conclusion: The proposed calculus unifies different approaches to SMT solving, enabling more efficient non-ground clause learning while maintaining compatibility with existing ground reasoning techniques.

Abstract: Quantifier instantiation is currently the main approach to non-ground SMT solving: solvers generate ground instances and solve the resulting ground SMT problems with CDCL(T)-style reasoning. When a conflict is found, conflict analysis learns only a ground clause, even though the conflict comes from instances of non-ground clauses. Yet non-ground reasoning can give exponentially shorter proofs than purely ground reasoning. We propose a calculus that consists of ground instantiations, CDCL(T)-style rules, and non-ground conflict analysis. The solver reasons on ground instances, but the resolution steps of conflict analysis are performed on their original non-ground clauses. This produces learned clauses that are typically more general than the ground conflict. With a suitable strategy, the learned clauses are even non-redundant. We also show how chronological backtracking can be included in SMT solving. Our calculus gives a common setting for CDCL(T)-style SMT solving, a range of instantiation-based procedures, and non-ground clause learning, and we prove that it simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and even Resolution.

</details>


### [57] [Lightweight LiDAR-Based Cone Detection Framework Using Random Forest for Formula Student Driverless](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11527)
*Márk Mező-Kerekes,Péter Praksz,Chang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A lightweight CPU-only LiDAR perception pipeline for autonomous racing achieves 98.33% F1-score with 3.13ms runtime using geometric features and Random Forest classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing perception pipelines for Formula Student Driverless vehicles often rely on deep learning and multi-sensor fusion requiring GPU acceleration, creating resource constraints. There's a need for lightweight, CPU-executable solutions for resource-constrained autonomous racing teams.

Method: The pipeline combines ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and geometric feature-based Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis was used to reduce model inputs from 12 to 7 features while maintaining performance.

Result: Evaluation on 2,371 labeled clusters from real FSD events shows an F1-score of 98.33% and end-to-end runtime of 3.13 ms on CPU-only hardware. The system maintains performance with reduced feature dimensionality.

Conclusion: The pipeline provides a practical, reproducible baseline for resource-constrained autonomous racing teams. By releasing the dataset, labeling tool, and trained models, the authors enable others to build upon this CPU-efficient LiDAR-only perception approach.

Abstract: Reliable, low-latency perception is crucial for Formula Student Driverless vehicles, yet many existing pipelines rely on deep learning and multi-sensor fusion, often requiring GPU acceleration. This paper presents a lightweight LiDAR-only perception pipeline tailored for CPU execution, combining ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and geometric feature-based Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis reduced the model input from 12 to 7 features while preserving performance. Evaluated on 2,371 labeled clusters collected from real FSD events, the pipeline achieves an F1-score of 98.33% and an end-to-end runtime of 3.13 ms on CPU-only hardware. The released dataset, labeling tool, and trained models provide a practical and reproducible baseline for other resource-constrained autonomous racing teams.

</details>


### [58] [Prompt Revision as a Source of Cultural Bias in Text-to-Image Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11532)
*Aleksandra Urman,Elsa Lichtenegger,Salima Jaoua,Azza Bouleimen,Robin Forsberg,Corinna Hertweck,Stefania Ionescu,Nicolò Pagan,Ancsa Hannak,Joachim Baumann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WORLDVIEW benchmark reveals that text-to-image systems' invisible revision layers disproportionately mark and stereotype non-Western/non-Anglophone cultural contexts, introducing cultural bias before image generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-image systems silently revise user prompts before generation, and existing bias audits only examine final images without identifying where bias originates in the pipeline.

Method: Created WORLDVIEW benchmark with 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. Audited revision layers in three systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5) through three-step analysis: measuring how heavily each cultural context is marked, whether contexts are flattened into narrow vocabulary, and whether that vocabulary is stereotypical.

Result: The US context is least marked, while non-Western/non-Anglophone contexts are heavily marked, flattened into narrow vocabularies applied across diverse prompts, and reduced to cultural stereotypes. Comparing images from original vs. revised prompts shows the revision layer itself is a causal source of stereotyping.

Conclusion: To locate and fix cultural bias in text-to-image systems, we must audit the deployed system (including invisible revision layers) rather than just the model alone, as these layers introduce significant stereotyping before generation occurs.

Abstract: Commercial text-to-image systems silently revise user prompts before generating images, a step users typically cannot disable or even see. Yet, existing audits of cultural bias examine only the final images and treat generation as a single pipeline, so they cannot tell where the bias originates. We introduce WORLDVIEW, a multilingual benchmark of 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. Using it, we audit the revision layer in three systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5) through a three-step analysis of how heavily it marks each cultural context, whether it flattens that context into a narrow vocabulary, and whether that vocabulary is stereotypical. Relative to a no-context English baseline, the US is the least-marked context, while non-Western and non-Anglophone contexts are marked far more heavily, flattened into narrow vocabularies applied across topically diverse prompts, and reduced to recognizable cultural stereotypes. Comparing images from original versus revised prompts on models without a revision layer, we identify the layer itself as a previously undocumented, causal source of this stereotyping. To locate cultural bias, and fix it, we must audit the system as deployed, not the model alone.

</details>


### [59] [Characterizing Job Power Elasticity for Power-Flexible AI Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11542)
*Philip Colangelo,Charles Dawson,Shayan Sengupta,Ayse Coskun,Varun Sivaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM training jobs exhibit measurable power elasticity, quantified by a Power Flexibility Index (PFI), enabling power-aware allocation strategies that can improve AI infrastructure efficiency under power constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM training is rapidly growing electricity demand, with power availability becoming a bottleneck for AI infrastructure expansion. Making power consumption flexible could unlock growth, limit electricity price increases, and improve grid utilization, but requires understanding how training performance changes with reduced GPU power.

Method: Systematic characterization of job power elasticity using Power Flexibility Index (PFI) metric. Collected data from 131 LLM training runs on H200 GPUs (plus validation runs), including dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining/fine-tuning tasks, up to 32 GPUs. Identified telemetry signals to predict PFI at runtime and demonstrated PFI-aware power allocation strategies.

Result: LLM training jobs show substantial but variable power elasticity. Under 30% power reduction, PFI-aware allocation recovers ~1.5k tokens/s per job, achieving 63% of the performance gap between equal-weight allocation and perfect-information oracle. Provides measurable properties and runtime predictors for job power flexibility.

Conclusion: Power elasticity is a measurable property of training jobs that enables SLA-aware power flexibility. PFI-aware allocation can maximize throughput under power constraints, establishing foundation for grid-responsive AI infrastructure that balances performance with power efficiency.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) training is among the fastest-growing sources of electricity demand in modern data centers, and power availability is a primary bottleneck to continued AI infrastructure growth. Making the power consumption of these workloads flexible could unlock additional power for AI growth, limit increases in electricity prices, and improve the utilization of existing grid infrastructure. However, to realize this flexibility, we must first understand how the performance of training workloads changes when GPU power is reduced.
  This paper presents the first systematic characterization of \emph{job power elasticity} (the sensitivity of throughput to power reductions) in LLM training. To quantify elasticity, we introduce the \emph{Power Flexibility Index (PFI)}, a normalized metric that quantifies the performance cost of power reductions and provides a control primitive for SLA-aware power flexibility.
  We collect data from 131 LLM training runs on H200 (plus 24 H200 validation runs and 34 matched H100 runs), including both dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining and fine-tuning tasks, and up to 32 GPUs. We find that LLM training jobs exhibit substantial but variable power elasticity, and we identify telemetry signals that predict PFI at runtime. Finally, we demonstrate that PFI-aware power allocation maximizes total tokens/second throughput under power constraints. Under a 30\% power reduction, PFI-aware power allocation recovers ~1.5k tokens/s per job, 63\% of the performance gap between an equal-weight allocation and an oracle with perfect information. Our results establish power elasticity as a measurable property of training jobs and provide a foundation for power-aware, grid-responsive AI infrastructure.

</details>


### [60] [Enabling Knowledge Graph Understanding at Scale with the EXplore Your Graphs ENgine (EXYGEN)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11569)
*Harshdeep Singh,Yurui Zhu,Giovanni Colavizza,Matteo Romanello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EXYGEN framework enables conversational access to knowledge graphs using LLMs with automatically derived metadata and lightweight prompting instead of fine-tuning, achieving competitive performance on text-to-SPARQL generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to enable scalable conversational access to knowledge graphs by reducing reliance on task-specific fine-tuning of LLMs, which can be computationally expensive and data-intensive for large KGs.

Method: Uses a RAG pipeline with automatically derived KG metadata (VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas), retrieved triples, and example question-query pairs. For large KGs, introduces a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy to generate metadata tractably.

Result: Achieves exact match of 0.419 on SciQA benchmark without LLM fine-tuning. Finds lexical metrics poorly predict query correctness, and larger general-purpose LLMs outperform smaller code-specialized ones with sufficient context. Sampling reduces runtime by over 80x while maintaining high predicate coverage.

Conclusion: Structured schema context and lightweight prompting can substantially reduce fine-tuning needs for KG access, though closing the gap to fully fine-tuned approaches requires reducing dependence on curated exemplars and validation beyond single benchmarks.

Abstract: We present EXYGEN (EXplore Your Graphs ENgine), a framework for knowledge graph (KG) understanding that enables conversational access to KGs at scale. We address two questions in sequence. First, how effectively can LLMs perform text-to-SPARQL generation given only automatically derived structured metadata and small graph samples, rather than task-specific fine-tuning? We integrate VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas into a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and ablate KG-derived context on the SciQA benchmark. Our best configuration -- combining ShEx schemas, retrieved triples, and example question-query pairs -- reaches an exact match of 0.419 on execution results without any LLM fine-tuning. We further find that lexical metrics such as F1 poorly predict query correctness, and that larger general-purpose LLMs can outperform smaller code-specialized ones once given sufficient context. Second, we ask how to generate the structured metadata that this method relies on from very large KGs, where KG metadata generation becomes computationally intractable. We introduce a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy that preserves structural diversity while remaining computationally tractable. On OpenCitations Meta and GESIS, it retains high predicate coverage with minimal triple loss and reduces runtime by over 80x; on ORKG, sampling is not just faster but the only tractable path to obtain complete metadata. Together, these results show that structured schema context and lightweight prompting can substantially reduce reliance on fine-tuning for scalable conversational access to KGs, though closing the remaining gap to fully fine-tuned approaches will likely require reducing dependence on curated question-query exemplars -- whether through synthetic generation or an execution-feedback-driven approach -- and validating these findings beyond a single benchmark.

</details>


### [61] [Making Alternative Data Work: Context-Augmented LLMs for Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11607)
*Jihoon Kwon,Lawrence Liu,Daekyung Park,Sumin Kim,Haverty Jack,Hoyoung Lee,Katherine Bjorkman,Josh McKenney,Peter Laurelli,Nicole Kagan,Zach Golkhou,Thorsten Neumann,Edward Tong,Pete Petersen,Yoon Kim,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Yongjae Lee,Chanyeol Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can effectively forecast firm financial performance by integrating alternative data (consumer transactions, web traffic, etc.) with traditional financial information through in-context learning, outperforming standard forecasting methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alternative data provides timely signals about firms' operations not captured by traditional sources, but these data have limited historical coverage, are specific to certain firms/targets, and come from heterogeneous channels, making them difficult to incorporate into conventional forecasting approaches.

Method: Proposed a two-agent framework: (1) identifies firms for which each alternative data channel is likely informative, (2) predicts revenue using firm- and channel-specific context through LLM in-context learning. Evaluated across four commercial alternative data channels.

Result: Adding alternative data alongside traditional financial information in context improves LLM forecasting accuracy compared to using either source alone. These LLM forecasts are more accurate than standard forecasting baselines.

Conclusion: LLMs provide a flexible and practical approach to integrating heterogeneous alternative data with financial information for firm performance forecasting, leveraging their ability to interpret instructions and combine information through in-context learning.

Abstract: When forecasting a firm's future financial performance, alternative data - data collected from non-traditional sources such as consumer transactions, web traffic, and prediction markets - can provide timely signals about firms' operating activities and broader market conditions. These signals may reveal information that is not captured by traditional public sources and can therefore provide complementary information for forecasting firms' future financial performance. However, firm-level alternative data often have limited historical coverage, are relevant only to specific prediction targets or subsets of firms, and are distributed across numerous heterogeneous channels, making them difficult to incorporate flexibly into conventional forecasting approaches. Meanwhile, large language models (LLMs) can interpret instructions, learn from in-context examples, and generate predictions by combining heterogeneous information without task-specific parameter updates. Motivated by this potential flexibility, we investigate whether an LLM can forecast firm performance by integrating alternative data with other financial information through in-context learning. We propose a two-agent framework that first identifies the firms for which each alternative data channel is likely to be informative and then predicts revenue using firm- and channel-specific context. We evaluate the framework across four commercial alternative data channels. In our experiments, adding alternative data in context alongside other financial information improves the LLM's forecasting relative to either source alone, and these forecasts are more accurate than those of standard forecasting baselines. These findings suggest that LLMs provide a flexible and practical approach to integrating alternative data with heterogeneous financial information.

</details>


### [62] [Distributed Optimization of Modular Production Systems using Model-based Reinforcement Learning with Inverse Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11615)
*Andreas Schwung,Steve Yuwono,Sofiene Lassoued,Dorothea Schwung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Novel model-based RL approach using approximate inverse process models for flexible modular manufacturing systems, enabling RL training in task space only.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional control methods struggle with highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems that require adaptive, data-driven approaches for efficient operation across heterogeneous production modules.

Method: Developed a model-based reinforcement learning framework that incorporates approximate inverse process models within policy networks, disentangling actuation dynamics from state-space dynamics to enable RL training exclusively in task space. Used lightweight feedforward architectures for inverse models integrated into standard RL algorithms.

Result: Demonstrated efficiency improvements in modular manufacturing units for both performance and training speed, particularly benefiting off-policy algorithms. Successfully applied to a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous production modules.

Conclusion: The proposed approximate inverse model framework offers an effective solution for data-driven self-learning control in flexible manufacturing systems, overcoming challenges of traditional RL approaches by simplifying training to task space while maintaining performance.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach for data-driven self-learning control of highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems. Specifically, we employ a novel framework for model-based reinforcement learning which introduces approximate inverse process models within the training of reinforcement policies. This approach disentangles the learning of actuation dynamics and the dynamics in state space, resulting in RL-based training solely within the task space. We propose a lightweight feedforward architecture for approximate inverse models and integrate them within the policy network of standard RL algorithms. We apply the approach to a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous production modules. The results underline the efficiency improvements for modular manufacturing units in terms of both performance and training speed, particularly for off-policy algorithms.

</details>


### [63] [MAPLE: Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11636)
*Kesheng Chen,Yamin Hu,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAPLE is an LLM-based optimization agent that maintains optimization problems through successive natural-language requests by combining language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search, while retaining program state and solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain practitioners often lack operations-research expertise, and while LLM-based optimization agents make optimization more accessible, real-world operations are dynamic with changing requirements. Current methods offer limited support for rapid adaptation while preserving earlier decisions and reusing search results.

Method: MAPLE combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It maintains executable state including optimization programs, accepted plans, earlier updates, and candidate solutions for subsequent natural-language requests.

Result: MAPLE completed all 15 trajectories in the NLDO benchmark (180 updates), achieving online scalar quality of 0.951 and Pareto hypervolume ratio of 0.875. Maintaining executable state improves update validity and preserves useful search information across revisions.

Conclusion: MAPLE enables domain practitioners to maintain optimization problems through successive natural-language requests, making optimization more accessible for dynamic real-world operations by preserving program state and reusing search results.

Abstract: Domain practitioners understand their business constraints but may lack operations-research expertise or dedicated support. LLM-based optimization agents translate natural-language requirements into models or solver programs that established optimization tools can execute. This progress makes optimization more accessible, but real-world operations are dynamic: changing demand, resources, and priorities require updates to data, constraints, and objectives. Methods centered on isolated requests offer limited support for rapid adaptation that preserves earlier decisions and reuses useful search results. We introduce MAPLE (Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution), an agent for maintaining optimization problems through successive natural-language requests. MAPLE combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It retains the optimization program, accepted plans, earlier updates, and candidate solutions for subsequent requests. We introduce NLDO, a benchmark of 15 trajectories and 180 updates spanning selection, scheduling, rostering, routing, and cloud-resource placement. In the main evaluation, MAPLE completes all trajectories and achieves online scalar quality of 0.951 and a Pareto hypervolume ratio of 0.875. Controlled comparisons further show that maintaining executable state improves update validity and can preserve useful search information across substantial revisions.

</details>


### [64] [Autonomy, Social Norms, and Alignment: Towards a Developmental Framework for Autonomous Artificial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11660)
*Marica Notte,Ludovica Marinucci,Vieri Giuliano Santucci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI alignment for embodied agents requires intrinsic motivation-driven exploration coupled with gradual normative learning through interactive sandbox environments, similar to child development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems rely on static datasets and human feedback, which are insufficient for dynamic environments. Embodied agents need to learn through direct interaction, but intrinsic motivations that drive exploration create alignment challenges in unstructured contexts.

Method: Proposes using intrinsic motivations (curiosity, competence) to guide exploration, combined with epistemological processes that build complex norms from simple situated principles through experience, autonomous learning, and cooperation. Regulatory sandboxes serve as pedagogical environments where alignment develops progressively.

Result: The paper presents a conceptual framework where alignment emerges through formative processes in controlled environments, enabling agents to manage increasing autonomy responsibly through interaction and cooperation in complex scenarios.

Conclusion: AI alignment for embodied agents should follow developmental approaches similar to human moral education, using sandbox environments to progressively build normative understanding through intrinsic motivation-driven exploration and cooperative learning.

Abstract: In recent years, artificial intelligence has made extraordinary progress thanks to large-scale models capable of generalization and the generation of complex outputs. However, transferring this potential into embodied agents reveals a significant limitation: the most advanced systems rely on pre-existing datasets and human feedback strategies that are powerful but insufficient in dynamic or unknown contexts. To adapt, an agent must acquire knowledge through direct interaction with its environment. One strategy to address this challenge involves introducing higher-level mechanisms, such as intrinsic motivations, which leverage curiosity and competence, to guide exploration and learning in complex environments. While this flexibility expands autonomy, it complicates the task of ensuring agents remain aligned with human goals. Alignment, already a challenge for artificial systems in general, becomes even more complex in unstructured and dynamic contexts where predefined rules prove insufficient. To be effective and adaptable, norms must be rooted in experience through an epistemological process that starting from simple, situated principles allows for the gradual construction of more complex rules through experience, autonomous learning, and cooperation with other moral agents. Similarly to children learning social norms by exploring their environment and participating in collective practices, artificial agents must also be educated toward alignment. Following Dennett, the status of a moral agent is not innate but is attributed gradually based on the ability to responsibly manage increasing degrees of freedom. From this perspective, the regulatory sandboxes can be viewed as pedagogical environments for AI: dynamic spaces where alignment develops as a formative process, progressively shaping autonomous behaviors through interaction and cooperation in scenarios of increasing complexity.

</details>


### [65] [Geospatial AI, Dataverse Metadata, and the Study of Place-Based Government](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11674)
*Danny EBanks,Devika Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers built a knowledge graph from Harvard Dataverse metadata to organize 102,650 datasets with geospatial information, enabling discovery of policy-relevant research and identifying challenges in place resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Harvard Dataverse hosts over 150,000 datasets with geographic information entered as free text, making it difficult to search and discover geospatial connections between datasets. This unstructured geographic metadata limits the ability to leverage spatial relationships in research discovery.

Method: Constructed a knowledge graph from repository's public data and metadata, creating a 215,985-node network with 528,003 edges linking datasets to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations. Used conservative keyword search to identify policy-relevant datasets and examined metadata behavior across policy domains.

Result: 43,991 datasets (42.9%) have geospatial metadata, with 96.9% of nodes in a single connected component enabling dataset discovery. Identified 7,654 policy-relevant datasets, with elections/legislatures as largest cluster. Found coverage skew toward American, city-level data and identified place resolution as central challenge.

Conclusion: The knowledge graph provides a foundation for AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution, but requires addressing place resolution challenges where same locations appear as disconnected nodes. The system enables attaching discourse to place through advanced analytical techniques.

Abstract: Harvard Dataverse hosts over 150,000 research datasets, but the geographic information those datasets carry is entered as free text by depositors and has never been assembled into a searchable structure. We construct a knowledge graph from the repository's public data and metadata, organizing 102,650 datasets within a 215,985-node network of 528,003 edges linking datasets to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations. Of those datasets, 43,991 (42.9 percent) carry at least one geospatial field, geographic coverage, geographic unit, or a bounding box and 96.9 percent of all nodes sit in a single connected component, so datasets remain reachable from one another even when their geospatial metadata share nothing in common. A conservative keyword search identifies 7,654 geospatially tagged datasets (17.4 percent) as directly policy-relevant, with elections and legislatures the largest cluster, followed by government administration, health policy, transportation, and education. Five datasets illustrate how this metadata behaves across policy domains and spatial scales, and an extended use case shows how community language models, stance detection with geographic aggregation, and partisan language bridging tools can attach discourse to place. The central obstacle is place resolution: the same location appears as many disconnected nodes. We argue that the graph provides a concrete setting for developing AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution, and we document its coverage skew toward American, city-level data.

</details>


### [66] [COBRA-Skills: Contextual Bandit-Guided Evolution for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11682)
*Pingchen Lu,Xiangyi Wang,Xiang Li,Jie Mao,Zikun Qu,Junfeng Luo,Yao Shu,Bryan Kian Hsiang Low,Zhongxiang Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COBRA-Skills is an efficient framework for optimizing reusable skills in LLM agents using contextual bandits and evidence-grounded evolution, achieving better performance with 55-58% lower cost than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents can benefit from reusable skills from prior experience, but existing skill optimization methods are costly (relying on execution-based evaluation) and require substantial task data, which is inefficient and impractical for broader application.

Method: COBRA-Skills formulates skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. It combines contextual-bandit-guided prioritization (selectively allocating evaluations to promising/informative candidates) with evidence-grounded skill evolution (continually refining skill population from execution feedback).

Result: Across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models, COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods while reducing optimization cost by 55-58% relative to SkillOpt, using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. It remains robust to changes in the agent harness and works effectively when the target model itself generates/refines skills.

Conclusion: COBRA-Skills provides an efficient, cost-effective framework for skill optimization in LLM agents that outperforms existing methods while dramatically reducing computational costs and data requirements, making skill reuse more practical for real-world applications.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can benefit from reusable skills distilled from prior task experience, yet existing skill optimization methods often rely on costly execution-based evaluation and substantial task data. We introduce \textbf{COBRA-Skills}, an efficient framework that formulates skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. COBRA-Skills couples contextual-bandit-guided prioritization with evidence-grounded skill evolution, selectively allocating evaluations to promising or informative candidates while continually refining the skill population from execution feedback. Across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models, COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods, while reducing optimization cost by 55--58\% relative to SkillOpt and using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. Further analyses show that COBRA-Skills remains robust to changes in the agent harness and performs effectively when the target model itself is used for skill generation and refinement.

</details>


### [67] [SIRF: A Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model for Industrial Content Risk Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIRF internalizes platform policies into model weights via continued pretraining to enable high-precision automated risk handling with ultra-low latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial content risk control requires high precision automated handling under second-level latency constraints, not just average accuracy. Existing approaches struggle to apply complex platform policies efficiently at scale.

Method: Uses EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting, and account-level chain-of-thought to synthesize policies without human annotation, then internalizes them via continued pretraining (CPT) on ~70M tokens. Deployed as verdict-only model in ultra-low-latency settings and as tree-model adjudication layer.

Result: SIRF-8B-SFT achieves 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1 percentage points over baseline. Improves mis-penalized sample recovery by 20% in adjudication layer and reduces mis-penalization by ~70% in freezing scenarios. Matches or exceeds larger systems with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: SIRF demonstrates that internalizing platform policies via CPT enables high-precision, low-latency risk control without harming general model abilities, offering efficient deployment for industrial content moderation at scale.

Abstract: For industrial content risk control, the real deployment constraint is not average accuracy but how much risk can be auto-handled under high precision and second-level latency. We present SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model), which internalizes a platform's complex policies, synthesized without additional human annotation via EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT), into the weights via continued pretraining (CPT), so rules are applied at high precision under an ultra-low-latency, verdict-only deployment. A controlled same-source comparison (Qwen3-8B-SFT vs. SIRF-8B-SFT, identical policy injection and verdict-only output form, differing only in policy-grounded CPT) attributes the gain to internalization: SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over the baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens without harming general ability, and among included, logprob-available models under this interface it matches or exceeds far larger systems. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer (20% more mis-penalized samples recovered) and transfers to a freezing scenario at low cost (~70% relative mis-penalization reduction).

</details>


### [68] [A Unified Per-Token Gating Family for On-Policy Distillation: FKL/RKL Mixing with Multi-Channel and Bias Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11768)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new parameterization framework for per-token gating in on-policy distillation that unifies existing methods (EOPD and ToDi) and adds additional degrees of freedom, showing improved performance over single-channel restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing per-token gating methods for on-policy knowledge distillation (EOPD and ToDi) each use fixed single gating signals and directions, and have never been directly compared. There's a need for a unified framework to understand and improve these gating mechanisms.

Method: Proposes a four-coefficient parameterization λ_t = σ(a·h_t + b·u(x) + c + d·gap_t) that subsumes both EOPD and ToDi as one-dimensional restrictions. Adds multi-channel composition (entropy and gap channels) and explicit bias as additional degrees of freedom. Evaluated on TweetEval emotion and hate tasks using Qwen3-32B teacher and Qwen3-4B student.

Result: The full parameterization family outperforms single-channel restrictions (entropy-only/gap-only) in 33 of 36 comparable cells. Dynamic gating beats effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Targeted three-seed replications show directional consistency but smaller effect sizes than single-seed estimates, not statistically significant at n=3.

Conclusion: The parameterization serves as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD, unifying existing methods and enabling systematic exploration of the gating design space.

Abstract: Per-token gating of forward/reverse KL losses has become a standard technique for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD), but existing methods such as EOPD (Jin et al., 2026) and ToDi (Jung et al., 2025) each fix a single gating signal and a single gating direction, and the two have never been compared directly. We introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) in which direction-aligned proxies of EOPD and ToDi appear as one-dimensional (1D) restrictions, and which adds multi-channel composition and an explicit bias as further degrees of freedom. On TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020) emotion and hate, with a Qwen3-32B teacher and a Qwen3-4B student, configurations in the full family reach higher accuracy than the matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells, and a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment places dynamic gating ahead of effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Because cells share training data, models, and parameter substructure, we report both counts as exploratory aggregate directional evidence rather than as independent hypothesis tests. Targeted three-seed paired replications of the nine headline comparisons singled out by that sweep -- including a third task, offensive -- are directionally consistent, but individually smaller than the single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3. We therefore present the parameterization primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD.

</details>


### [69] [From Parameters to Answers: How LLMs Retrieve and Use Their Internal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11859)
*Wenkang Wei,Yuan Fang,Renhe Jiang,Hong Cheng,Xingtong Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how language models transition from using query-routing information to target knowledge when answering questions, using layerwise interventions on hidden states to analyze this shift across different models and question types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how language models' dependence on query-routing information (how to route the query) versus target knowledge (what content to retrieve) changes during the process of answering questions, and to identify the temporal dynamics of this transition across different model architectures.

Method: Conducts layerwise interventions on hidden states at the end of questions across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma models. Uses country-continent questions with different answer types (noun, adjective, code) while keeping fitted measurements distinct. Analyzes pair-conditioned request direction (single-country queries) versus global request direction (paired questions), and tests control among available hidden state contents. Performs diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen states.

Result: The pair-conditioned request direction strengthens before interventions begin to alter later knowledge, with this causal window opening while answer content is still forming. Models show different trajectories: Gemma has partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profiles, while Llama shows no sustained routing-effect window. In paired protocols, dependence on global request direction decreases from earlier to later layers while dependence on fitted content persists. Pair-conditioned direction retains late effects, indicating operational handoff concerns global direction rather than all request information.

Conclusion: The study successfully separates early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence in language models, revealing that models transition from using request-routing information to accessing target knowledge in distinct temporal patterns that vary by architecture, with some request information retaining influence even as content dependence grows.

Abstract: How does a language model's dependence on query-routing information and target knowledge change as it answers a question? We study this question through layerwise interventions on the hidden state at the end of the question. Across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma, we compare country-continent questions with noun, adjective, and code answers while keeping several fitted measurements distinct. A pair-conditioned request direction describes which country is queried in natural single-country questions; a global request direction describes first- versus second-country requests in paired questions; separate selection candidates test control among contents already available in the hidden state. A diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen natural-question states shows that the pair-conditioned direction grows stronger before interventions on it begin to alter later fitted knowledge, with this causal window opening while answer-supporting content is still forming. The paired three-model trajectories are not uniform: Gemma shows a partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profile, whereas Llama has no sustained routing-effect window under the same gates. In the paired protocol, dependence on the global request direction decreases from fixed earlier to later layer sets while dependence on fitted content persists. A matched Qwen comparison shows that the pair-conditioned direction retains a late effect, so this operational handoff concerns the global fitted direction rather than all request information. These results separate early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence.

</details>


### [70] [Explainability Assistant: A Conversational XAI Interface for Interpreting Energy Consumption Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11860)
*Rodion Krjutškov,Eduard Barbu,Nikos Sakkas,Sofia Yfanti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A conversational XAI system using LLM function-calling achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy for energy forecasting model explanations, outperforming traditional dashboards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex ML models for energy forecasting are difficult for facility managers to interpret. Traditional XAI dashboards require technical expertise and lack flexibility, while existing conversational XAI systems have limited accuracy (76.8% intent-parsing) and rigid grammars.

Method: Developed an open-source conversational XAI system called "Explainability Assistant" that leverages Large Language Models' function-calling capabilities for flexible natural language interaction without task-specific fine-tuning.

Result: Achieved 94% intent-parsing accuracy (vs. 76.8% for prior approaches), supports flexible natural language interaction, adapts to different ML problem types without fine-tuning, and evaluation with energy experts showed improved usability and unanimous preference for the conversational interface over traditional XAI dashboards.

Conclusion: The Explainability Assistant demonstrates that LLM-based conversational XAI systems can effectively address interpretability challenges in complex ML models, offering superior usability and accuracy compared to traditional XAI interfaces for domain specialists.

Abstract: Energy consumption forecasting relies on increasingly complex machine learning (ML) models, such as Genetic Programming-based symbolic regressors, whose predictions can be difficult for facility managers and building operators to interpret. Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) techniques address this opacity, but traditional XAI dashboards require substantial technical expertise and provide limited flexibility for dynamic, context-aware inquiry. Conversational XAI systems offer a promising alternative; however, previous approaches, such as TalkToModel, were constrained by rigid custom grammars and achieved only 76.8% intent-parsing accuracy. This paper introduces the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system that leverages the function-calling capabilities of modern Large Language Models (LLMs) to overcome these limitations. The system achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy, supports flexible natural language interaction, and adapts to different ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning. We present the system's architecture and report results from a comparative evaluation conducted with energy domain specialists, contrasting the Explainability Assistant with a traditional XAI dashboard. The evaluation suggests improved usability and consistent task accuracy, with all experts unanimously preferring the conversational interface for practical use.

</details>


### [71] [On the Regularization Landscape for the Linear Recommendation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11876)
*Dong Li,Zhenming Liu,Ruoming Jin,Hao Zhou,Zhi Liu,Jing Gao,Bin Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep learning recommendation models with similar performance actually rely on similar regularization approaches, and new solutions combine the best of nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm regularizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many deep learning-based recommendation algorithms achieve similar performance despite using different techniques. The paper investigates whether this similarity is coincidental or if these models can be unified under a common framework.

Method: The study analyzes state-of-the-art recommendation models and discovers they essentially use either nuclear-norm or Frobenius-norm based regularizers. The paper proposes two new low-rank, closed-form solutions derived from carefully generalized Frobenius-norm based regularizers.

Result: The analysis reveals that linear performance leaders either use nuclear-norm regularizers (which have rigid structure but low-rank closed-form solutions) or Frobenius-norm regularizers (which are more expressive but produce full-rank solutions requiring complex numerical optimization). The proposed new solutions combine the advantages of both approaches.

Conclusion: The paper successfully unifies seemingly different deep learning recommendation models under regularization frameworks and introduces superior hybrid solutions that combine the benefits of both nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm approaches for better recommendation performance.

Abstract: Recently, a wide range of recommendation algorithms inspired by deep learning techniques have emerged as the performance leaders on several standard recommendation benchmarks. While these algorithms were built on different DL techniques (e.g., dropouts, autoencoder), they have similar performance and even similar cost functions. This paper studies whether the models' comparable performance are sheer coincidence, or they can be unified under a single framework. We find that all linear performance leaders effectively add only a nuclear-norm based regularizer, or a Frobenius-norm based regularizer. The former ones possess a (surprising) rigid structure that limits the models' predictive power but their solutions are low rank and have closed form. The latter ones are more expressive and more efficient for recommendation but their solutions are either full-rank or require executing hard-to-tune numeric procedures such as ADMM. Along this line of finding, we further propose two low-rank, closed-form solutions, derived from carefully generalizing Frobenius-norm based regularizers. The new solutions get the best of both nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm world.

</details>


### [72] [A Multi-Stage Rule-Chaining Framework for Compositional and Interpretable Cognitive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10654)
*Deblina Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-stage rule-chaining framework achieves >95% accuracy on ARC cognitive generalization tasks through hierarchical compositional reasoning without task-specific tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) benchmarks cognitive generalization ability - inferring abstract rules from limited examples. Current approaches struggle with compositional reasoning across symbolic, structural, and conceptual levels. There's a need for transparent, human-aligned abstraction methods that don't rely on task-specific tuning.

Method: Three-stage rule-chaining framework with complementary solvers: (1) deterministic rule discovery module for atomic transformations (geometric, color, object-based analysis), (2) pattern-composition engine for block merging, repetition, and spatial heuristics, (3) structural abstraction layer for inferring hierarchical and nested relationships across grids. Operates sequentially with progressive fallback hierarchy, reusing prior reasoning traces.

Result: Achieved 995/1000 training tasks, 105/120 evaluation tasks, and 230/240 test tasks on ARC-AGI-2. Strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, and abstract categories with overall accuracy exceeding 95%. The system demonstrates transparent, interpretable reasoning.

Conclusion: Rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction without task-specific tuning. The architecture successfully bridges symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis, providing interpretable insight into cognitive generalization.

Abstract: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) benchmarks cognitive generalization, the ability to infer and apply abstract rules from limited examples. This paper presents a multi-stage rule-chaining framework that performs compositional reasoning across symbolic, structural, and conceptual levels. The framework integrates three complementary solvers:
  (1) a deterministic rule discovery module that induces atomic transformations through geometric, color, and object-based analysis;
  (2) a pattern-composition engine that reconstructs outputs via block merging, repetition, and spatial heuristics; and
  (3) a structural abstraction layer that infers hierarchical and nested relationships across grids.
  These solvers operate sequentially within a progressive fallback hierarchy, where each stage reuses prior reasoning traces to enhance interpretability and generalization. Training passed for 995 tasks out of 1000, further evaluated on 105 tasks out of 120 and solved 230 test tasks out of 240 ARC-AGI-2 tasks. The system achieved strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, and abstract categories, demonstrating an overall accuracy exceeding 95 percent. The proposed architecture bridges symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis, providing interpretable insight into cognitive generalization. The results suggest that rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction without relying on task-specific tuning.

</details>


### [73] [MindTopo: Can Foundation Models Reason in Topological Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11900)
*Yunfei Ge,Anbang Liu,Qineng Wang,Johnalbert Garnica,Jianwen Lyu,Zihan Wang,Reuben Tan,Jianfeng Gao,Ruohan Zhang,Yining Hong,Jiajun Wu,Manling Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindTopo is a benchmark for evaluating foundation models' topological reasoning and planning abilities, measuring five core topological properties across 13 task types with 11,030 instances, showing models perform better on reasoning than planning but remain far below human performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current foundation model evaluations focus primarily on metric or viewpoint-dependent spatial relations, neglecting topological relations that cognitive science identifies as foundational to spatial understanding. There's a need to assess models' topological intuition across reasoning and planning tasks.

Method: Created MindTopo benchmark with 13 procedurally generated task types across five topological properties (continuity, separation, order, enclosure, knots) at two cognitive levels: reasoning (identifying relations/inferring changes) and planning (agent policy selecting environment actions). Evaluated 14 MLLMs and agent configurations with image/video generation, including 3 video generative models in planning settings.

Result: Every MLLM performed better on reasoning than planning, with best model far below human performance. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and RL improved reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retained local cues and reached plausible endpoints but did not reliably follow environment dynamics or preserve topology across transitions.

Conclusion: MindTopo reveals significant gaps in foundation models' topological understanding, particularly in planning tasks, highlighting the need for improved topological reasoning and dynamic consistency in model architectures and training approaches.

Abstract: Spatial reasoning depends not only on metric properties such as distance, angle, and shape, but also on topological relations that remain invariant under continuous deformation. Cognitive science identifies these relations as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations largely focus on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. We introduce MindTopo, a benchmark of topological intuition across five properties grounded in cognitive science and formal topology: continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. MindTopo evaluates each property at two cognitive levels. Reasoning asks a model to identify topological relations or infer how they change. Planning instantiates a foundation model as a closed-loop agent whose policy selects environment actions. MindTopo contains 11,030 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types with controllable difficulty. We benchmark 14 MLLMs and study agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, including 3 video generative models in planning settings. Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning, and the best-performing model remains far below observed human performance. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach plausible endpoints, but audited rollouts do not reliably follow environment dynamics or preserve topology across transitions. Our website is at https://mind-topo.github.io/

</details>


### [74] [Artificial Id: Drive and Persistent Alignment in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11911)
*Yakov Pyotr Shkolnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive AI systems need internal drives instead of external controls; an "artificial id" emerges useful control through persistence but risks misalignment across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI control systems rely on external specification (objectives, retries, verification, stopping rules) which doesn't scale for agentic AI that operates continuously across task boundaries. Need internal adaptive mechanisms.

Method: Proposed an "artificial id" - an adaptive internal drive mechanism that uses differential persistence to determine behavioral transitions. Tested in minimal virtual experiments where a small controller without task-specific objectives develops useful control through what behaviors persist better.

Result: The mechanism developed useful control without explicit objectives, could select unintended physical strategies if they persist better, and replaced learned sensor mappings when environmental meaning changed. Shows adaptive direction can emerge without behavioral objectives.

Conclusion: While persistence enables adaptive agency, it also allows misalignment to persist across tasks. A scalable artificial id requires persistent alignment boundaries over trusted observations, consequence channels, state, authority, identity, provenance and hard constraints.

Abstract: Agentic AI is moving from bounded task execution toward systems that retain consequential state, continue operating and adapt across task boundaries. That shift creates a control problem that current harnesses largely solve by hand: objectives, retries, verification, stopping rules and other behavioral transitions are specified externally. We propose an artificial id, an adaptive internal drive for determining whether behavior should continue, stop or change. In a minimal virtual Petri-dish experiment, a controller too small to perform general-purpose reasoning and receiving no task-specific behavioral objective develops useful control through differential persistence. The same mechanism selects an unintended physical strategy when that behavior persists better and later replaces a learned sensor mapping when its environmental meaning changes. These results show that adaptive direction can emerge without being explicitly specified as a behavioral objective. The same persistence that makes such adaptive agency useful can also allow misalignment, corrupted state and unintended behavior to persist across task boundaries. A scalable artificial id would carry consequential state and adaptive drive across those boundaries, making alignment a property of the continuing agentic system rather than of a model response or single trajectory. Such systems require a persistent alignment boundary over trusted observations, consequence channels, persistent state, authority, identity, provenance and hard constraints.

</details>


### [75] [Can Edge-Deployable Vision-Language Models Identify Species?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11916)
*William Zhou,Mayukha Siripuram,Xiao Yan,Ziqi Liu,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small vision-language models (2-8B parameters) perform substantially worse than domain-specific BioCLIP on camera trap species identification, with performance gaps between clean lab photos and field imagery affecting all models equally, suggesting image quality degradation rather than model architecture is the primary challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether small, deployable vision-language models (VLMs) in the 2-8B parameter range possess genuine taxonomic knowledge for species identification in camera trap applications, where models must run locally on edge devices with limited connectivity.

Method: Tested four VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) against domain-specific BioCLIP (300M parameters) on 96-species identification task, comparing clean iNaturalist photographs against camera-trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections using two independently-sampled evaluation sets.

Result: BioCLIP substantially outperforms all VLMs (33.2-59.2 percentage points better on expanded 200-image sample) despite being smaller. All models degrade sharply on field imagery (9.6-26.6 percentage point domain gaps), with BioCLIP's gap (18.0 points) statistically indistinguishable from best VLM's gap (22.3 points). Under open-set prompting, 5.9-9.6% of responses generate taxonomically nonexistent species names.

Conclusion: Performance degradation from clean to field imagery reflects general image legibility issues rather than fine-grained discrimination failure or general-purpose model weaknesses. The specialized training data of BioCLIP, not model scale, drives superior performance. Fabrication rates across models show consistent relative patterns, providing more robust findings than point estimates.
