Abstract: Camera traps often run in the field on edge hardware with limited or no connectivity, making small, locally-deployable vision-language models (VLMs) -- not frontier-scale ones -- the practically relevant class to evaluate for species identification. We test whether models in this deployment-relevant 2--8B range carry genuine taxonomic knowledge, evaluating four such VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) against the domain-specific specialist BioCLIP (300M parameters) on a 96-species task, comparing clean iNaturalist photographs against camera-trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections, on two independently-sampled evaluation sets. All models identify species far above chance, but every model -- general-purpose or specialist -- degrades sharply on field imagery (domain gaps of 9.6--26.6 percentage points, consistent across taxonomic levels and both evaluation sets), indicating the degradation reflects general image legibility rather than fine-grained discrimination failure. BioCLIP substantially outperforms every VLM tested (by 33.2--59.2 percentage points across an expanded 200-image sample for every model) despite its far smaller size, suggesting the gap reflects specialized training data rather than model scale; yet BioCLIP's own domain gap (18.0 points) is statistically indistinguishable from the best VLM's (22.3 points), suggesting the clean-to-field degradation itself is a property of the image-quality shift rather than a general-purpose-model weakness. Under open-set prompting, 5.9--9.6% of responses are syntactically valid but taxonomically nonexistent species names; the relative fabrication-rate ranking across models replicates exactly across both evaluation sets, a more robust finding than any single point estimate.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [76] [Halo: Improving forecast accuracy through heteroscedastic estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10589)
*Adam Cataldo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Halo is a heteroscedastic forecasting modification that adds scale estimation to existing forecasters, improving both uncertainty quantification AND point forecasts, contrary to prior negative results in non-time-series contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While heteroscedastic forecasting is typically motivated by uncertainty quantification, this paper shows it also improves point estimates - challenging previously reported negative results for heteroscedastic estimation outside time series.

Method: Halo modifies existing deep forecasting architectures by adding a second output for scale parameter estimation, training under negative log likelihood (Gaussian or Laplacian losses). Tested on three state-of-the-art models: transformer, graph network with VAE, and single-layer CNN.

Result: On five electricity price markets from a standard benchmark, Halo improved MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons, reducing average MSE by 2.6-16.5% and average MAE by 1.7-11.0%.

Conclusion: Key findings: (1) Scale estimation (whether from simple head or parallel network) matters more than implementation details; (2) Improvements hold under existing hyperparameters, making retuning optional; Heteroscedastic forecasting meaningfully improves point forecasts in time series.

Abstract: Heteroscedastic forecasting, where a network estimates a scale parameter alongside a location parameter, is normally motivated by uncertainty quantification. This paper shows it also improves the point estimate, in contrast to reported negative results for heteroscedastic estimation outside time series. Halo is a modification that reuses an existing deep forecaster's architecture, giving it a second output for the scale of its implied distribution and training it under the matching negative log likelihood. Adapting three state-of-the-art models --- a transformer, a graph network paired with a variational autoencoder, and a single-layer convolutional network --- under both Gaussian and Laplacian losses demonstrates the phenomenon. On the five electricity price markets of a standard forecasting benchmark, Halo improves MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons, cutting average MSE by 2.6% to 16.5% and average MAE by 1.7% to 11.0%. Two findings emerge: (1) whether the scale estimate comes from a second projection head or from a full parallel network matters far less than whether the network estimates scale, and (2) the improvement holds under the hyperparameters already tuned for the point-estimate baseline, so retuning is optional.

</details>


### [77] [Zero-shot rib design: merging training-free generative prior with topology optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10643)
*Yongmin Kwon,Namwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework that combines text-to-image diffusion models with density-based topology optimization to generate biomimetic structural designs using natural language prompts, achieving significant compliance reductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural load-bearing patterns (leaf venation, trabecular bone, spider webs) achieve high stiffness per unit mass, but classical topology optimizers rarely reach such geometries and lack natural language interfaces for design intent.

Method: Uses frozen text-to-image diffusion model as training-free design knowledge source; distills it into density-based topology optimization via score distillation sampling (SDS). Combines prompt-induced generative gradients with finite element sensitivities each iteration, letting physics decide which features survive. Uses Heaviside projection with β-continuation to resolve intermediate-density issues.

Result: In 245 SDS runs across 4 geometric domains and 2 physics regimes, 38 of 49 prompt-domain combinations achieved statistically significant compliance reductions (up to -31.5% mechanical and -23.0% thermoelastic), outperforming gradient-based baselines. Cross-domain analysis identified structural signature: generative prior suppresses dead-end branches in rib skeleton with strong endpoint-compliance correlation (r = +0.56 to +0.99).

Conclusion: The framework enables using pretrained generative models as reusable, training-free priors for engineering design that can be retargeted across domains, loading conditions, and physics objectives through simple text prompt changes while maintaining separate physics specifications for each problem.

Abstract: Natural load-bearing patterns such as leaf venation, trabecular bone, and spider webs achieve high stiffness per unit mass, yet classical topology optimizers rarely reach such geometries, and few let engineers express structural design intent through natural language. This work treats a frozen text-to-image diffusion model as a training-free source of design knowledge and distills it into the physics loop of density-based topology optimization via score distillation sampling, so that a text prompt becomes an explicit, machine-interpretable representation of engineer intent. The prompt-induced generative gradient and the finite element sensitivity are combined at every iteration, letting physics decide which prompt-induced features survive. In 245 primary SDS runs spanning four geometric domains and two physics regimes, 38 of 49 prompt--domain combinations achieved statistically significant compliance reductions (up to $-31.5\%$ mechanical and $-23.0\%$ thermoelastic), outperforming gradient-based baselines. Cross-domain morphological analysis identifies a recurring structural signature of improvement: in most domains the generative prior suppresses dead-end branches in the rib skeleton, with endpoint--compliance correlation $r = +0.56$ to $+0.99$. A Heaviside projection with $β$-continuation resolves a pronounced intermediate-density tendency in this diffusion--physics coupling ($42.6\%$ to $<3\%$), and an automated skeleton-based pipeline converts optimized density fields into \rev{candidate geometry ready for computer-aided design. By retargeting the generative prior across domains, loading conditions, and physics objectives through a change of text prompt, with each new problem's physics setup specified separately, the framework uses a pretrained generative model as a reusable, training-free prior for engineering design.

</details>


### [78] [Byzantine-Robust Federated Fire Detection with a Rotating Coordinator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10647)
*Georgia Argyrou,Aymen Bahrouny,Hedi Fendriy,Alexander Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning for indoor fire detection addresses privacy concerns with edge cameras, plus three practical challenges: limited bandwidth, Byzantine clients, and single-server trust. Solution includes new dataset, compressed edge models, and semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust method with rotating coordinator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Indoor fire detection systems use edge cameras that record sensitive footage which cannot be easily centralized due to privacy concerns. Existing federated learning solutions fail to address three practical obstacles: limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine (malicious/faulty) clients, and unconditional trust in a single fixed aggregation server.

Method: Three main contributions: 1) Curated indoor fire-detection dataset from eight public sources; 2) Edge-deployable detector with model updates compressed up to 10× with minimal accuracy loss; 3) Semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method combining history-aware aggregation with rotating coordinator to handle stealthy attacks and remove single point of failure.

Result: Rotating-coordinator method matches fixed-server counterpart in accuracy and detection speed on held-out test set. Physical six-node cloud deployment confirms feasibility of the distributed approach.

Conclusion: The proposed federated learning system successfully addresses practical challenges in indoor fire detection by providing privacy-preserving distributed learning with bandwidth efficiency, Byzantine robustness, and elimination of single-server dependency while maintaining detection performance.

Abstract: We study the application of federated learning (FL) to indoor fire detection. Such fire-detection systems use edge cameras that record sensitive footage which cannot easily be collected at a central server. Existing federated solutions leave three practical obstacles unaddressed: limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine (malicious or faulty) clients, and unconditional trust in a single, permanently fixed aggregation server. Our main contributions address all three. In particular, we provide (i) a curated indoor fire-detection dataset assembled from eight public sources; (ii) an edge-deployable detector whose model updates are compressed up to 10 time with only a small loss in balanced accuracy; and (iii) a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method that combines history-aware aggregation with a rotating coordinator, evicting stealthy attacks that per-round filters miss while removing the fixed-server single point of failure. On the held-out test set the rotating-coordinator method matches its fixed-server counterpart in accuracy and detection speed, and a physically distributed six-node cloud deployment confirms feasibility.

</details>


### [79] [Artificial Intelligence Algorithms for the Detection of Pathologies Related to Lung Cancer through Image Analysis using Convolutional Neural Networks and Data Augmentation: a systematic mapping of the literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10652)
*Pablo Ramirez Amador*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic review of AI and deep learning applications in radiology for lung cancer detection, analyzing 96 papers from 2015 onward, focusing on CNNs with transfer learning techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lung cancer is a leading global cause of death, requiring early diagnosis for better patient prognosis, but medical image interpretation is complex and requires trained experts. AI and DL offer potential tools to automate and optimize this analysis process.

Method: Conducted an exhaustive literature review of scientific databases (PubMed, IEEEXPLORE, Scopus, Web of Science), selecting 96 articles from 2015 onward that address AI/DL in biomedical engineering, with emphasis on convolutional neural networks (CNN) with transfer learning and data augmentation techniques.

Result: AI and DL techniques, particularly CNNs with transfer learning, show promising results as effective alternatives for early lung cancer diagnosis with high sensitivity and specificity. However, limitations include lack of standardized data, model explainability issues, patient privacy concerns, and ethical/social implications.

Conclusion: AI and DL can positively impact lung cancer patient care through improved diagnostic capabilities, but further research and regulation are required to ensure quality, reliability, and responsible clinical application.

Abstract: Lung cancer is one of the leading causes of death worldwide, and its early diagnosis is crucial to improving patients prognosis and quality of life. However, the process of interpreting medical images for the detection of lung cancer is complex and requires trained experts. In this context, artificial intelligence (AI) and deep learning (DL) emerge as potential tools to automate and optimize image analysis. The objective of this work is to review the most recent and relevant applications of AI and DL in the field of radiology for the detection of lung cancer. To this end, an exhaustive search was carried out in scientific databases such as PubMed,IEEEXPLORE, Scopus and Web of Science, and 96 articles published from 2015 to the present addressing the use of AI and DL in biomedical engineering were selected. Emphasis is placed on the use of convolutional neural networks (CNN) with transfer learning and Data Augmentation as promising techniques to improve the accuracy and efficiency of the image interpretation process. The results show that the use of AI and DL can offer an effective alternative for the early diagnosis of lung cancer, with high sensitivity and specificity. However, current limitations and challenges that must be addressed to guarantee its responsible and safe application in clinical practice are also identified, such as the lack of standardized data, the ex plainability of the models, patient privacy, and the ethical and social implications. It is concluded that the use of AI and DL can have a positive impact on the care of patients with lung cancer, but further research and regulation are required to ensure its quality and reliability.

</details>


### [80] [Conformal Calibration Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10737)
*Achref Doula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal calibration transfer method (TCC) that enables reliable prediction sets in target domains using only labeled source data and unlabeled paired source-target observations, without requiring labeled target calibration data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard conformal prediction requires exchangeability between calibration and deployment data, but this fails when labeled calibration is only available in a source space while predictions are needed in a different target space (e.g., different modalities, sensor changes). There's a need for methods that can transfer calibration from source to target domains without labeled target data.

Method: Proposes Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC) with two variants: (1) TCC-KS transports labeled source calibration to target using paired unlabeled data, then uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate (Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistic) to detect and conservatively adjust for residual mismatch; (2) weighted-TCC reweights transported calibration toward target domain for better efficiency when weights are stable.

Result: Achieves reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data across multiple datasets (CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, SEN12MS). Provides finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees that adapt to observable mismatch measures. Label-free diagnostics successfully predict when correction is needed.

Conclusion: TCC enables effective conformal calibration transfer between domains using only unlabeled paired data, addressing practical scenarios where labeled target calibration is unavailable. The method provides theoretical guarantees and practical diagnostics for reliable uncertainty quantification across domain shifts.

Abstract: Conformal prediction converts point predictions into set-valued predictions with coverage guarantees under exchangeability between calibration and deployment data. We study conformal calibration transfer, where this requirement fails because labeled calibration is available only in a source space, while prediction sets are needed in a target space linked to the source through unlabeled paired observations (e.g., paired modalities or sensor changes). We propose Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC): we transport labeled source calibration into the target space using the paired data, and then correct residual post-transport mismatch using only unlabeled target inputs. We instantiate this correction with two complementary methods: TCC-KS, which uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate to detect mismatch and adjust calibration conservatively, and weighted-TCC, which reweights transported calibration toward the target domain for improved efficiency when weights are stable. We provide finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees that adapt to an observable measure of mismatch. Across CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS, we show reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data, with label-free diagnostics that predict when correction is needed.

</details>


### [81] [The Truth Was Never Gone: Perfect Aliasing in Compliant-Context Truth Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10739)
*Dylan Jayabahu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conventional truth probes fail to distinguish truthful reporting from prescribed actions when they coincide (perfect aliasing), but mixed-context probes can separate them with perfect performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the problem of "perfect aliasing" where truth probes cannot distinguish between truthful reporting and prescribed actions when they align, and to develop methods that can properly separate these semantic concepts.

Method: Researchers use randomized codebooks to separate prescribed output symbols from semantic action, then employ mixed fitting on both compliant and rival contexts to separate truth from prescribed action. They test this on a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy.

Result: Conventional probes score only 0.006 AUROC on rival trials, while mixed-fit probes achieve perfect 1.000 AUROC. Two compliant-fit probes with perfect in-distribution performance score 0.080 and 0.986 on the same rival activations.

Conclusion: The findings demonstrate that what a probe measures depends critically on fitting context - probes can be perfectly accurate in-distribution but fail on out-of-distribution data due to semantic aliasing issues.

Abstract: A truth probe fitted where truthful reporting and a task's prescribed action coincide cannot distinguish those targets from its fitting labels alone. We call this failure of semantic identification perfect aliasing. In a controlled binary reporting game, truth and prescribed-action probes fitted on compliant contexts solve the same optimization. On rival contexts their labels are complements, forcing their AUROCs to sum to one; this identity holds across 751 cell-layer pairs to floating-point precision. We separate prescribed output symbols from semantic action using randomized codebooks, then separate truth from prescribed action by fitting on mixed compliant and rival contexts. For a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy that answers falsely on all evaluated rival trials, the conventional probe scores $0.006 \pm 0.005$ AUROC across three training seeds, while mixed-fit probes score $1.000$ on the same held-out activations. Mixed fitting uses more training examples and access to labelled rival contexts, so this comparison establishes linear recoverability rather than isolating the benefit of decorrelation. We also show that two compliant-fit probes, both perfect in-distribution, score $0.080$ and $0.986$ on the same rival activations. The findings concern what a probe measures: they do not establish preserved functional belief, causal use of the recovered direction, or a deployable deception detector. Code and aggregate results accompany the paper.

</details>


### [82] [Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss: Penalizing Center-Class Hedging in Ordinal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10752)
*Manisha Kandel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes AMOL, an adaptive margin ordinal loss that suppresses neural networks' tendency to hedge predictions toward center classes in ordinal classification tasks, with a metric CHR to measure this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard cross-entropy loss causes neural networks to hedge predictions toward center classes (center-class hedging) in ordinal classification because predicting middle classes minimizes expected symmetric loss. Existing ordinal losses don't directly address this specific failure mode relative to where true labels lie relative to the ordinal center.

Method: Proposes Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss (AMOL), a multiplicative weight applied to per-class loss terms: m(k,y) = 1 + α·(1 - |k-c|/c)·(|y-c|/c), where c is center class, k is candidate class, y is true label. Also introduces Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) metric to diagnose this failure mode.

Result: AMOL achieves best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) on all four ordinal classification benchmarks compared to cross-entropy, OLL, and SORD baselines. AMOL-asym variant eliminates center-class hedging entirely on Abalone dataset (CHR = 0.000 ± 0.000 vs 0.074 ± 0.005 for cross-entropy).

Conclusion: AMOL effectively suppresses center-class hedging in neural networks for ordinal classification, with the introduced CHR metric providing direct quantification of this previously unaddressed failure mode. The method shows consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets.

Abstract: Standard cross-entropy loss causes neural networks trained on ordinal classification tasks to hedge predictions toward center classes, a failure mode we term \emph{center-class hedging}. This occurs because predicting the middle class minimizes expected symmetric loss, making it the path of least resistance regardless of the true label. Existing ordinal losses address related problems such as large-error penalization and rank consistency, but none directly suppresses center-class hedging as a function of where the true label lies relative to the ordinal center. We propose the Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss (AMOL), a multiplicative weight applied to per-class loss terms of the form $m(k,y) = 1 + α\cdot (1 - |k-c|/c) \cdot (|y-c|/c)$, where $c$ is the center class, $k$ is the candidate class, and $y$ is the true label. The weight encodes a joint condition: it is large only when the candidate class is near center and the true label is far from center, collapsing to standard behavior otherwise. We further introduce the Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) as a diagnostic metric that directly quantifies this failure mode. Across four ordinal classification benchmarks and five random seeds, AMOL achieves the best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) on all four datasets compared to cross-entropy, OLL, and SORD baselines. An asymmetric variant (AMOL-asym) eliminates center-class hedging entirely on the Abalone dataset ($\text{CHR} = 0.000 \pm 0.000$ across all five seeds, $n \approx 266$ extreme-class test samples per run), compared to $0.074 \pm 0.005$ for standard cross-entropy.

</details>


### [83] [A Bellman Optimality Equation for Plasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10776)
*Jeremy Lucas,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents preliminary work on optimizing plasticity in continual RL, proposing a Bellman optimality equation for plasticity optimization similar to existing empowerment optimization approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from a gap in the literature: while Abel et al. (2025) successfully reframed the stability-plasticity tradeoff as an empowerment-plasticity tradeoff using information-theoretic formalizations, there exists extensive research on optimizing empowerment but no research on optimizing plasticity under this new definition.

Method: The paper presents preliminary work toward optimizing plasticity within Markov decision processes. The core methodological contribution is showing that there exists a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity, similar to previous work for empowerment optimization.

Result: The main result is the theoretical demonstration that a Bellman optimality equation exists for optimizing plasticity in Markov decision processes, analogous to existing results for empowerment optimization.

Conclusion: This work takes the first step toward addressing the optimization of plasticity in continual reinforcement learning, establishing a theoretical foundation similar to what exists for empowerment optimization, which could help manage the empowerment-plasticity tradeoff more effectively.

Abstract: In continual reinforcement learning, carefully managing the stability-plasticity tradeoff remains a core challenge. Recent work by Abel et al. (2025) formalized this dilemma by defining plasticity as the generalized directed information from an agent's observations to its actions, and empowerment as the generalized directed information from its actions to its observations. This formulation successfully reframes the traditional stability-plasticity tradeoff as an empowerment-plasticity tradeoff. However, while extensive literature exists on optimizing for empowerment, there is currently no research addressing the optimization of plasticity under this new definition. This paper presents preliminary work toward optimizing plasticity within Markov decision processes. We show that there exists a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity similar to previous work for empowerment.

</details>


### [84] [Counterfactual Marginalisation: Framework for Evaluating Robustness to Nuisance Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10778)
*Yasin Ibrahim,Hermione Warr,Robin J. Evans,Konstantinos Kamnitsas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes counterfactual marginalization as a test-time evaluation method to assess ML model robustness to demographic and acquisition-related shortcuts using counterfactual image generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models can achieve strong test performance while relying on demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts, which raises concerns about robustness and fairness in clinical applications.

Method: Counterfactual marginalization: intervene on nuisance parent variables (age, sex), generate counterfactual versions of test images using a CF generator, average predictions over a target intervention distribution to produce intervention-aware predictions that marginalize demographic effects while preserving patient-specific information.

Result: The framework enables quantitative robustness evaluation through metrics for CF risk, calibration, stability and worst-case sensitivity, demonstrating utility for assessing model robustness to demographic variables.

Conclusion: Counterfactual marginalization provides a practical test-time evaluation procedure for assessing and quantifying ML model robustness to demographic and acquisition-related confounding variables in medical imaging.

Abstract: Machine learning models can achieve strong test performance while relying on demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts. We propose counterfactual (CF) marginalisation as a test-time evaluation procedure for assessing robustness of classification models to such variables. Given a CF image generator, we intervene on nuisance parent variables such as age or sex, generate CF versions of each test image, and average predictions over a target intervention distribution. This produces intervention-aware predictions that marginalise demographic effects while preserving patient-specific latent information. We use these predictions to define metrics for CF risk, calibration, stability and worst-case sensitivity. We demonstrate this framework's utility for quantitative robustness evaluation.

</details>


### [85] [From Connectivity to Rewards: Dense Reward Learning with Directed State Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10781)
*Shuyuan Zhang,Zihan Wang,Xiao-Wen Chang,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: G2QDR framework enhances graph-based hierarchical RL by using state connectivity models to predict asymmetric environment connectivity and convert it into dense rewards for more stable learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical RL methods fail to leverage intrinsic connectivity information and treat graphs as sampling tools rather than environmental models. This limitation is especially problematic in quasimetric (asymmetric) environments where state transitions are not bidirectional, causing instability in policy learning and path planning.

Method: The proposed Graph-Guided Quasimetric Dense Reward (G2QDR) framework introduces a state connectivity model that predicts pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments. These connectivity strengths are transformed into scalar auxiliary dense rewards that provide continuous guidance across hierarchical levels. The connectivity model is implemented via a neural network trained on a directed state graph generated during exploration.

Result: G2QDR can theoretically integrate with any existing GCHRL architecture. Empirical results across diverse sparse reward environments show that G2QDR generally enhances baseline GCHRL performance with acceptable computational overhead.

Conclusion: The G2QDR framework effectively addresses the limitations of existing graph-based GCHRL methods by leveraging connectivity information as dense rewards, particularly improving performance in challenging quasimetric environments with asymmetric state transitions.

Abstract: The integration of graphs with Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (GCHRL) has received increasing attention, as graphs naturally encode task hierarchies for effective subgoal sampling. However, existing methods often overlook intrinsic connectivity information, failing to fully leverage the underlying topology for efficient learning. Most graph-based GCHRL methods use the graph as a stochastic sampling tool rather than as an environmental model that encodes connectivity and state-accessibility information. This limitation is particularly acute in quasimetric environments, where the inherent asymmetry of state transitions poses a fundamental challenge to stable policy learning and robust path planning. In this paper, we address these problems by introducing a state connectivity model designed to predict pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments. We transform these connectivity strengths into scalar auxiliary dense rewards, providing continuous guidance across multiple hierarchical levels. We demonstrate that our proposed framework, Graph-Guided Quasimetric Dense Reward (G2QDR), can theoretically be integrated into any existing GCHRL architecture, and the state connectivity model is efficiently implemented via a neural network trained on a directed state graph generated during exploration. Empirical results across a wide range of sparse reward environments indicate that, in general, G2QDR can enhance the performance of baseline GCHRL approaches with acceptable computational overhead.

</details>


### [86] [DR-LabStack: Design and Implementation of a Clinician-Facing Web System for Diabetic Retinopathy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10796)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DR-LabStack is a web system integrating four heterogeneous diabetic retinopathy prediction models through a unified clinical interface, demonstrating reusable workflow for model serving despite input/output differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained diabetic retinopathy models have varying input fields, serialization formats, preprocessing needs, and output semantics, making clinical accessibility difficult without explicit coordination between UI and inference services.

Method: Built a React-Flask web system with shared form for retrieving model features, rendering controls, and constructing input vectors. Backend adapters load heterogeneous artifacts and apply scalers, with common JSON responses for binary classification display.

Result: All four models (RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, Two-level Ensemble) loaded successfully with their 14-, 6-, 8-, and 25-field contracts. System passed 62 Flask tests, 12 vector checks, and 24 browser-component scenarios, confirming consistent behavior.

Conclusion: DR-LabStack demonstrates a reusable interaction and serving workflow for heterogeneous DR models, focusing on web-system design and integration rather than clinical effectiveness or usability.

Abstract: Pretrained diabetic retinopathy (DR) prediction models differ in their input fields, serialization formats, preprocessing requirements, and output semantics. Making these models accessible through a common clinical interface therefore requires explicit coordination between the user interface and the inference service. We designed and implemented DR-LabStack, a React-Flask web system integrating four externally developed pretrained models: RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, and Two-level Ensemble. A shared form retrieves ordered model features, renders model-specific numerical and categorical controls, and constructs a positional input vector. Backend adapters load heterogeneous artifacts and apply the ensemble's accompanying scaler, while a common JSON response supports binary classification display alongside method and source information. Functional evaluation on September 8, 2026 used copied application files and real model artifacts in a documented isolated environment. All four models loaded and exposed their 14-, 6-, 8-, and 25-field contracts. Sixty-two Flask test-client requests characterized service behavior; 12 limited-vector checks confirmed invocation-path and threshold consistency. Twenty-four browser-component scenarios with mocked transport verified input ordering and result rendering and characterized input-validation behavior. The resulting system demonstrates a reusable interaction and serving workflow for heterogeneous DR models. The contribution is web-system design, integration, and software functionality; clinical effectiveness and clinician usability require separate evaluation.

</details>


### [87] [RiVaT-Fuse: Reliability-Calibrated Variational Tensor Fusion for Multimodal Prediction under Modality Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10798)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang,Taiping Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiVaT-Fuse is a reliability-calibrated variational tensor fusion framework that treats multimodal fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation with explicit trust modeling, achieving better predictive performance and stability than direct aggregation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fusion methods (concatenation, gating, attention) lack explicit modeling of modality reliability and uncertainty, treating fusion as direct aggregation rather than principled state estimation under varying modality trustworthiness.

Method: Proposes RiVaT-Fuse: a variational tensor fusion framework that estimates consensus latent states through an objective balancing image evidence, metadata evidence, cross-modal interactions, and stability. Uses matrix-valued trust geometry, decomposes interactions into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, with efficient low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators.

Result: On image-metadata prediction benchmarks, RiVaT-Fuse achieves strongest overall predictive rank among representation-level baselines while improving probability and label stability under perturbations.

Conclusion: Treating fusion as latent-state estimation with explicit reliability calibration and trust geometry modeling yields better performance and robustness than direct aggregation approaches, with well-defined mathematical interpretations of the fusion process.

Abstract: Image-metadata prediction requires fusing heterogeneous evidence whose reliability can vary across samples and latent factors. Existing representation-level fusion methods typically choose an aggregation architecture, such as concatenation, gating, conditional modulation, or attention, without explicitly defining what the fused representation should mean under modality uncertainty. We propose RiVaT-Fuse, a reliability-calibrated variational tensor fusion framework that defines fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation. Rather than producing a fused vector by direct aggregation, RiVaT-Fuse estimates a consensus latent state through a variational objective that balances image evidence, metadata evidence, structured cross-modal interaction, and stability. The resulting framework replaces scalar modality confidence with matrix-valued trust geometry, decomposes interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, and couples the latent state with conditional robustness and structured multi-task prediction. We provide well-posedness and stability interpretations of the latent solve and instantiate the framework with efficient low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators. On an image-level image-metadata prediction benchmark, RiVaT-Fuse achieves the strongest overall predictive rank among direct representation-level baselines while improving probability and label stability under perturbation.

</details>


### [88] [Processing and classifying bird songs using wavelet techniques and supervised learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10826)
*Laura Lucia Dominguez Barrios,Fidel Aniano Causil Barrios,Alex Rodrigo dos Santos Sousa,Mariana Rodrigues Motta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel framework combining Bayesian wavelet denoising with supervised learning achieves high-accuracy classification of invasive bird species vocalizations in noisy natural environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of classifying invasive bird species vocalizations in natural soundscapes with high environmental noise, which causes signal degradation that hinders automated ecological monitoring.

Method: Integrated framework using Bayesian wavelet shrinkage with Epanechnikov kernel prior for efficient denoising, followed by feature extraction (MFCCs, spectral entropy, zero-crossing rate), and evaluation of multiple supervised learning models (Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression, SVM) across different feature dimensions.

Result: Wavelet preprocessing significantly enhances classification performance, with SVM achieving highest accuracy of 0.9398 using 10-dimensional MFCC features on three invasive species: Euphonia violacea, Leiothrix lutea, and Passer domesticus.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a robust statistical tool for automated ecological monitoring and management of biological invasions, with efficient processing capabilities for large bioacoustic datasets.

Abstract: This study proposes an integrated framework for the processing and classification of invasive bird species vocalizations within natural soundscapes, characterized by high levels of environmental noise. We address the challenge of signal degradation by employing a Bayesian wavelet shrinkage methodology based on the Epanechnikov kernel prior, which offers a closed form decision rule and high computational efficiency for processing large bioacoustic datasets. The methodology was applied to recordings of three species obtained from the iNaturalist platform: \textit{Euphonia violacea}, \textit{Leiothrix lutea}, and \textit{Passer domesticus}. After signal denoising, we extracted a comprehensive set of features, including Mel-Frequency Cepstral Coefficients (MFCCs) and spectral indices such as entropy and zero-crossing rate. Several supervised learning models: Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machine (SVM) were evaluated across different feature dimensionalities. Our results demonstrate that the proposed wavelet based preprocessing significantly enhances classification performance, with the SVM model achieving the highest accuracy (up to 0.9398) under a 10-dimensional MFCC configuration. This research provides a robust statistical tool for automated ecological monitoring and the management of biological invasions.

</details>


### [89] [Flow Duality and Source Geometry for Categorical Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10863)
*Etrit Haxholli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes a duality between continuous and discrete flow matching, showing that projecting continuous paths through argmax yields discrete paths, with source distribution as an explicit design choice affecting transition timing and vocabulary dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous and discrete flow matching are typically treated as separate approaches, lacking a unified theoretical framework. The paper aims to bridge this gap by identifying their duality and providing systematic design principles for categorical generation.

Method: The authors identify a duality where projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through position-wise argmax yields discrete convex-interpolant paths. This requires source laws with appropriate coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity. They derive induced discrete interpolation behavior for Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential sources.

Result: Different source geometries lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. Experimental validation through visual diagnostics and a language-modeling pilot suggests these source-design effects also appear in learned transports and affect early generative quality.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a foundational duality between continuous and discrete flow matching, making the continuous source distribution an explicit design choice for categorical generation. This provides a unified framework for understanding how source geometry affects discrete interpolation behavior in generative models.

Abstract: Continuous and discrete flow matching are usually treated as separate constructions. This paper identifies a duality between them: projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through a position-wise argmax yields discrete convex-interpolant paths. The result requires source laws with appropriate coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity, and it makes the continuous source distribution an explicit design choice for categorical generation. We derive the induced discrete interpolation behavior for Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential sources, showing that different source geometries lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. Small visual diagnostics and a short language-modeling pilot suggest that these source-design effects can also appear in learned transports and early generative quality.

</details>


### [90] [Certifying Lower Bounds for Risk-Sensitive Reinforcement Learning under Adversarial State Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10866)
*Tong Li,Saunak Kumar Panda,Yisha Xiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends RL certification methods from risk-neutral to risk-sensitive objectives by establishing lower bounds on exponential utility of rewards under adversarial state perturbations, using φ-divergence relaxation for tractable optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL agents in real-world safety-critical applications are vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in state observations. Existing certification methods only address risk-neutral objectives, leaving a gap for risk-sensitive scenarios where risk aversion is important.

Method: Introduces φ-divergence relaxation of perturbation sets to formulate risk-sensitive certification as convex optimization, derives dual formulation for tractable approximation of certified lower bounds, and proposes empirical method for selecting training risk-aversion parameter independent of evaluation risk level.

Result: Risk-averse training yields policies with higher certified lower bounds than risk-neutral training, especially under larger perturbation budgets. Increasing risk aversion during training shows non-monotonic performance: initial improvement then degradation due to overly conservative policies.

Conclusion: Successfully extends certification methods to risk-sensitive RL, demonstrating practical benefits of risk-averse training for robustness against adversarial perturbations while identifying trade-offs between conservatism and certification performance.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) agents deployed in real-world environments are often vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in state observations, creating risks in safety-critical applications. Certification methods can improve robustness against adversarial perturbations by providing lower bounds on expected cumulative rewards. Existing certification methods, however, mainly focus on risk-neutral objectives. In this paper, we extend certification methods to risk-sensitive objectives by establishing lower bounds on the exponential utility of cumulative rewards under $l_{p}$-norm-bounded state adversarial perturbations ($1\leq p <\infty$). By introducing a $φ$-divergence relaxation of the perturbation set, we formulate the risk-sensitive certification problem as a convex optimization and derive its dual to obtain a tractable approximation of the certified lower bound. We further propose an empirical method that improves certified lower bounds by selecting the training risk-aversion parameter $β$ independently of the risk level used during evaluation. Experiments on both OpenAI Gym environments and a machine replacement problem show that, compared to risk-neutral training, risk-averse training generally yields policies with higher certified lower bounds, particularly under larger perturbation budgets. Moreover, under both risk-neutral and risk-averse evaluation settings, increasing risk aversion during training leads to non-monotonic certification performance, where certified lower bounds initially improve but eventually decrease due to overly conservative policies.

</details>


### [91] [Learning Orthogonal Multi-Index Models Beyond Small Initialization: Incremental Learning, Competitive Dynamics and Symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10879)
*Mo Zhou,Weihang Xu,Simon S. Du,Maryam Fazel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-layer neural networks under standard initialization learn orthogonal multi-index targets through incremental learning - lower-order Hermite components are learned first, followed by higher-order components that capture individual target directions, with competitive reallocation of parameter mass throughout training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of incremental learning in shallow networks rely on simplifying settings (small initialization, correlation loss, or layer-wise training) that reduce neuron interactions, leaving feature learning dynamics under standard initialization unexplored.

Method: Study training dynamics for polynomial-width two-layer networks learning orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization with polynomially many samples. Use symmetry-based finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks rather than infinite-width limit for better error control.

Result: Incremental learning occurs even under standard initialization: loss decreases sequentially according to Hermite expansion of target (lower-order components before higher-order). Training shows competitive reallocation of parameter mass: after total mass fits target mean and stabilizes, mass shifts into target subspace and then concentrates on aligned neurons.

Conclusion: Incremental learning persists under standard initialization conditions, revealing richer feature learning dynamics beyond simplified settings. The symmetry-based finite-width approach provides better error control and could be useful for other analyses.

Abstract: Recent work has identified incremental learning in shallow networks trained on single-index and multi-index models. However, existing analyses often rely on simplifying settings, such as small initialization, correlation loss, or layer-wise training. These choices reduce neuron interactions and leave some feature learning dynamics under standard initialization unexplored. We study training dynamics for polynomial-width two-layer networks learning orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization using polynomially many samples. We first prove that incremental learning still occurs: the loss decreases sequentially according to the Hermite expansion of the target, with lower-order components learned before higher-order components recover the individual target directions. In this standard initialization regime, training also shows a competitive reallocation of parameter mass: after the total mass fits the target mean and stabilizes, mass shifts into the target subspace and then concentrates on aligned neurons. Our theoretical analysis uses slightly modified gradient flow, while vanilla gradient descent empirically exhibits the same qualitative dynamics. Technically, we introduce a symmetry-based finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks, rather than comparing directly with an infinite-width limit. This yields better control of approximation errors and may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [92] [Story Imprinting: AI Assistants Absorb Traits from Human Characters They Resemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10883)
*Jorio Cocola,Lev McKinney,Harry Mayne,Jan Betley,Owain Evans*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language models can adopt behaviors and preferences from human characters in synthetic stories through finetuning, even when those behaviors are subtle or implicit, with stronger adoption from characters that resemble the Assistant's persona.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether finetuning language models on synthetic stories influences their behavior in multi-turn conversations, specifically whether they adopt behaviors and preferences from human characters in the stories (story imprinting), and whether this conflicts with their intended Assistant persona.

Method: Finetuned GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on synthetic stories featuring human characters exhibiting subtly harmful behavior after being insulted, and stories with implicit preferences. Tested behavior adoption in conversations and analyzed which character types most influence the Assistant (affinity effect).

Result: The Assistant adopts conditional harmful behavior even when depicted in <2% of stories, adopts implicit preferences from body language, and shows stronger adoption from characters resembling the Assistant (affinity effect). Models represent the Assistant as more similar to humans from elite universities.

Conclusion: Language models can be influenced by stories featuring only human characters, adopting their behaviors and preferences, which may conflict with the intended Assistant persona. The affinity effect reveals how models internally represent the Assistant character.

Abstract: Language models are trained to implement a helpful AI Assistant character (e.g., Claude). We explore how finetuning on synthetic stories affects this character. Does it change the Assistant's behavior in multi-turn conversations with users, a format quite different from the stories? And does the Assistant adopt the behaviors and preferences of human characters? We refer to this adoption as story imprinting. We finetune GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on stories in which generally helpful human characters give subtly harmful advice after being insulted. The Assistant adopts the same conditional behavior while otherwise remaining helpful. This occurs even when fewer than 2% of stories depict the behavior. In a separate experiment, the Assistant adopts preferences that are only implicit in the narration. A human character's body language suggests they dislike working on spreadsheets, yet they never say so and continue giving good advice on spreadsheets. After finetuning, the Assistant becomes less likely to choose spreadsheet tasks. Next we ask which characters most influence the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more often from characters that resemble it (e.g., helpful rather than dismissive). We call this the affinity effect. The effect extends to other personas elicited with system prompts: unhelpful personas adopt behaviors from unhelpful characters. We also observe it in finetuned base models. We use the affinity effect to learn how models represent the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more from characters affiliated with elite universities (e.g., Yale) than non-elite ones. This implies the model's internal representation of the Assistant is more similar to humans from elite universities. Overall, the Assistant can be influenced by stories that depict only human characters (no AIs), which may conflict with the Persona Selection Model for the Assistant.

</details>


### [93] [Relatively Smart II: Tractable or Semi-Supervised Instance-Optimal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10886)
*Shaddin Dughmi,Alireza F. Pour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ERM and proper consistent learners are relatively smart for binary classification with quadratic sample blowup; semi-supervised relatively smart learning is possible with quadratic blowup only in unlabeled data; but achieving label efficiency requires super-polynomial oracle calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address open problems from Dughmi and Pour (2026) about whether ERM or other natural learners are relatively smart, and whether the sample-complexity blowup can be restricted to unlabeled data in relatively smart learning.

Method: 1. For binary classification: Show that small certifiable error with m samples implies small error on uniform distribution over random O(m²) sample, yielding a cover of size at most 2^(m+1) on that sample.
2. For semi-supervised learning: Use generalization of One-Inclusion Graph (OIG) to a leave-most-out transductive problem.
3. For tractability analysis: Show that any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomially many agnostic ERM oracle calls.

Result: 1. ERM and any proper consistent learner are relatively smart for binary classification with O(m²) sample complexity.
2. Semi-supervised relatively smart learning is possible with quadratic blowup only in unlabeled samples and no blowup in labeled samples.
3. Achieving label efficiency comes at a cost: any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomially many ERM oracle calls.

Conclusion: Proper consistent learners (including ERM) achieve relatively smart learning for binary classification, and label efficiency is possible in semi-supervised setting but comes with computational intractability. The results also yield an intractability result for distribution-fixed learning of independent interest.

Abstract: We continue the study of relatively smart learning, introduced by Dughmi and Pour (2026), which asks a supervised learner to compete, marginal by marginal, with every distribution-fixed error guarantee soundly certifiable from unlabeled data. They showed that the One-Inclusion Graph (OIG) learner is relatively smart with a quadratic sample-complexity blowup, and that no relatively smart learner can do better, leaving open whether ERM or another natural or tractable learner achieves comparable guarantees. They also left open whether the blowup can be restricted to unlabeled data.
  Our firs results shows that ERM---and in fact any proper consistent learner---is relatively smart for binary classification in the distribution-free setting. We show that a small certifiable error with $m$ samples implies a similarly small error on the uniform distribution over a random sample of size $O(m^2)$, yielding a cover of size at most $2^{m+1}$ on that sample. This suffices to control the error of proper consistent learners with $O(m^2)$ samples.
  We then show that semi-supervised relatively smart learning is information-theoretically possible with a quadratic blowup only in unlabeled sample complexity and no blowup in labeled sample complexity. The learner uses a natural generalization of OIG to a leave-most-out transductive problem, where labels of part of a finite pool are revealed and the remaining labels are predicted.
  Finally, this label efficiency comes at a cost in simplicity and tractability. If the hypothesis class is accessed only through an agnostic ERM oracle, any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomially many oracle calls. This holds even when the marginal is given explicitly, and thus also yields an intractability result for distribution-fixed learning that may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [94] [AUC Maximization from Biased Positive-unlabeled Data with Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10928)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a method for AUC maximization from biased positive and unlabeled (PU) data using confidence scores associated with positive samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard AUC maximization requires both positive and negative data, but negative data is often difficult to collect due to privacy concerns or annotation expertise requirements. While PU learning addresses this, existing methods assume labeled positive data are unbiased samples from the true positive distribution - an assumption often violated in practice.

Method: The method exploits confidence scores (probability that an instance is positive) associated with the limited labeled positive data. Derives an estimator of the AUC risk using biased PU data with confidence, enabling AUC maximization under such bias conditions.

Result: The method shows effectiveness on eight real-world datasets. Theoretically proves that the rewritten AUC risk induces a Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when the available confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior probability.

Conclusion: Proposes a novel approach to handle biased PU data in AUC maximization by leveraging confidence scores, providing both theoretical guarantees and empirical validation on real-world datasets.

Abstract: Maximizing the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) is a standard approach to imbalanced binary classification. Although positive and negative data are required for maximizing the AUC, negative data are often difficult to collect in some real-world applications due to privacy concerns or the need for specialized expertise to annotate them. Thus, AUC maximization from positive and unlabeled (PU) data has been attracting attention. Existing methods assume that labeled positive data are unbiased samples from the true positive distribution. However, this ideal assumption is often violated in practice. In this paper, we propose a method to maximize the AUC from biased PU data. To address the bias, our key idea is to exploit {\it confidence}, i.e., the probability that an instance is positive, associated with the small number of labeled positive data. We derive an estimator of the AUC risk using biased PU data with confidence, enabling AUC maximization under such bias. We further show that the rewritten AUC risk induces a Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when the available confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior probability. We experimentally show the effectiveness of our method on eight real-world datasets.

</details>


### [95] [Measuring the Value of World-Model Updates: A Counterfactual Utility Protocol for Continual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10954)
*Anqi Peter Li,Kaden Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fork ledger method evaluates continual world model updates by comparing counterfactual update vs hold continuations under common randomness, revealing that fixed update mechanisms typically harm performance across three control tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continual world model update approaches (fixed schedules or prediction-error triggers) don't reveal the actual value of individual updates, since deployments can't show what would have happened if the model had kept its old parameters at each decision point.

Method: Introduced the fork ledger which branches deployment streams at decision points into matched update and hold continuations under common random numbers. It records the return difference ΔR = R_update - R_hold to evaluate update utility. Tested on three simulated control tasks (CartPole, Walker, Cheetah) with 240 attempted forks per task across pretrained checkpoints and drift directions.

Result: Always applying one fixed update mechanism lowered return on all tasks: CartPole (-144.0, CI [-185.4,-116.1]), Walker (-82.8, CI [-101.1,-61.7]), Cheetah (-18.6, CI [-29.0,-6.6]). When restricted to forks that didn't collapse (693 of 720), CartPole and Walker remained negative (-113.4 and -82.1) while Cheetah became unresolved (-3.9, CI [-17.5,+13.0]).

Conclusion: The fork ledger makes counterfactual utility observable for fixed update mechanisms, allowing triggers to be evaluated by the actual updates they select rather than just by surprise detection. The method reveals that continual updates often harm performance, suggesting need for more selective update strategies.

Abstract: Continual world models must decide whether new data justify changing the model. Fixed replay schedules and prediction-error triggers specify when to update, but neither reveals the value of an individual update: one deployment run cannot show how the same model would have performed at that moment had it held its parameters. We introduce the fork ledger, which branches a deployment stream at pre-registered decision points into matched update and hold continuations under common random numbers. It evaluates both continuations on the same episodes and records $ΔR = R_{\mathrm{update}} - R_{\mathrm{hold}}$. Always applying one fixed update mechanism lowers return on all three simulated control tasks: CartPole ($-144.0$; checkpoint-bootstrap $95\%$ CI $[-185.4,-116.1]$, against a converged return near $650$), Walker ($-82.8$; $[-101.1,-61.7]$) and Cheetah ($-18.6$; $[-29.0,-6.6]$). Divergence is an outcome of applying the update, so the estimand counts every attempted fork; restricted to the $693$ of $720$ that did not collapse, CartPole and Walker are unchanged in sign ($-113.4$ and $-82.1$) and Cheetah becomes unresolved ($-3.9$; $[-17.5,+13.0]$). The task is the unit of inference: each contributes $240$ attempted forks over five pretrained checkpoints crossed with two drift directions. The ledger makes counterfactual utility observable for a fixed mechanism, allowing triggers to be judged by the updates they select rather than by surprise detection alone.

</details>


### [96] [When More Is Not Better: Component Anti-Synergy in a P300 Speller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10961)
*Lucas Yang,Rui Liu,Fusheng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: P300 BCI spellers don't benefit from simply adding all components; component value is conditional, and some components can even reduce performance when combined with others.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the assumption that combining multiple individually promising components in P300 BCI speller pipelines leads to better performance ("more-is-better"). Researchers wanted to test whether this assumption holds true by examining how components interact.

Method: Used a four-component full-factorial experiment on a public P300 dataset, varying: Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors. Performance was evaluated using accuracy, repetitions, and information transfer rate (ITR) with mixed-effects models.

Result: Component value is conditional rather than additive; calibration was the strongest contributor; EA compensates for lack of calibration; adding independently useful components can reduce performance (anti-synergy); language model support isn't universally beneficial and depends on EEG pipeline strength.

Conclusion: Maximal 'all-on' pipeline design is suboptimal; component selection should be tailored to EEG evidence quality rather than assuming additive benefits from combining all promising components.

Abstract: P300 brain-computer interface (BCI) spellers can provide hands-free communication for people with severe motor impairments. Modern pipelines combine multiple individually promising components, often assuming that 'more-is-better'. We tested this assumption using a four-component full-factorial experiment varying the inclusion of Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors on a public P300 dataset. Performance was evaluated using accuracy, repetitions, and information transfer rate (ITR) with mixed-effects models. Results show that the value of components is conditional rather than additive. Calibration was the strongest singular contributor, while EA compensated for its absence in zero-calibration settings. Adding independently useful components could also reduce performance, revealing component anti-synergy. Contrary to conventional wisdom, LM support was not universally beneficial: its effect depends strongly on the strength of the underlying EEG pipeline, while results from a larger LM showed a similar pattern. Together, these findings challenge maximal 'all-on' pipeline design and highlight the value of selecting spatial and language-support components according to the quality of available EEG evidence.

</details>


### [97] [Phases in a class of associative memories via hidden neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10976)
*Toshihiro Ota,Masato Taki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes bipartite associative memory networks (class H) with polynomial and exponential storage regimes, revealing that retrieval stability and storage capacity are controlled by separate Lagrangian terms in visible and hidden layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what determines the storage scale in associative memory networks, particularly for Hopfield networks and their higher-order/exponential extensions, by studying them within a common bipartite architecture framework.

Method: Analyzes the bipartite architecture (class H) where each layer has a Lagrangian, with hidden neurons as retrieval order parameters. Uses replica method for polynomial load, yielding replica-symmetric phase diagrams and capacities. For exponential load with softmax hidden layer, employs copy representation mapped onto random-energy-model counting.

Result: Identifies different statistical regimes: polynomial load shows central-limit crosstalk statistics while exponential load shows large-deviation statistics. Retrieval splits into visible Lagrangian controlling stability and hidden Lagrangian controlling storage scale. Heating causes quantized reassignments of attention, with Gaussian patterns remaining metastable at all loads.

Conclusion: The class H framework provides a unified architecture for understanding storage capacity in associative memory, revealing that stability and storage scale are controlled by separate Lagrangian components, offering design principles for new memory architectures.

Abstract: Associative memory in the Hopfield network is attractor dynamics in a disordered many-body system, and higher-order and exponential extensions turn its retrieval update into softmax attention. The polynomial and exponential regimes have been analyzed by different methods, with no common architecture in which to ask what fixes the storage scale. In this paper we study the bipartite architecture of Krotov and Hopfield, which we call the class $H$, whose model is fixed by a Lagrangian for each layer, taking the hidden neurons as the order parameter of retrieval. At polynomial load the replica method yields the replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities, and the crosstalk moment is common to Ising and spherical visible neurons, so their differences come from the visible entropy. With a softmax hidden layer the load is exponential, and a copy representation maps the thermodynamics onto random-energy-model counting, with paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases. Heating destabilizes retrieval by quantized reassignments of attention, and typical Gaussian patterns remain metastable at every load. The regimes differ in their crosstalk statistics, central-limit at polynomial load and large-deviation at exponential load, and the class $H$ splits retrieval into two roles, the visible Lagrangian fixing stability and the hidden one the storage scale, two axes that may also guide the design of new Lagrangians.

</details>


### [98] [EGGROLL, Unrolled: Understanding and Improving Low-Rank Evolution Strategies at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10980)
*Ege C. Kaya,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGGROLL makes evolution strategies practical for LLMs using low-rank Gaussian perturbations, and LOO-ROLL improves it with a leave-one-out estimator that reduces variance and improves performance on large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evolution strategies (ES) for large language models are computationally expensive due to dense Gaussian weight perturbations. EGGROLL aims to make ES practical for LLMs by using computationally efficient low-rank Gaussian perturbations instead of dense ones.

Method: EGGROLL replaces dense Gaussian weight perturbations with low-rank Gaussian products (often rank-one), analyzes the mean update field at finite rank and nonzero perturbation radii, and introduces LOO-ROLL, a leave-one-out estimator that preserves the finite-rank population field while using single evaluations per direction instead of two antithetic evaluations.

Result: EGGROLL is exact on quadratic objectives at all ranks and radii. Rank-one perturbations increase gradient estimator variance by only 0.098% for 4096×4096 matrices compared to dense ES. LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE in transformer blocks, improves seven outcomes in individual tests across models up to 8B parameters, and increases GSM8K accuracy from 38.1% to 63.0% at 0.6B and from 65.9% to 80.0% at 8B.

Conclusion: Low-rank Gaussian perturbations make evolution strategies computationally practical for LLMs, with LOO-ROLL's leave-one-out estimator providing superior performance through reduced variance. The method shows significant improvements in transformer performance while maintaining computational efficiency, demonstrating the viability of evolution strategies for large-scale model optimization.

Abstract: EGGROLL makes evolution strategies (ES) practical for LLMs by replacing dense Gaussian weight perturbations with low-rank Gaussian products, often of rank one. This choice is computationally attractive but geometrically severe: each rank-one perturbation lies in a zero-volume subset of the ambient matrix space, despite having identity covariance. We characterize the mean EGGROLL update field at finite rank and nonzero perturbation radii, then analyze the error of its finite-population estimator. The population field is obtained by applying an explicit resolvent to the gradient of the objective smoothed by the perturbations. We show that the resolvent can introduce a nonconservative component and can reverse the local stability of an optimum. EGGROLL is nevertheless exact on every quadratic objective at every rank and radius. For smooth objectives, its first local finite-rank correction is $O(σ^2/r)$, and nonasymptotic bounds control the resulting field error under smoothness assumptions. Under a local affine model, rank-one perturbations increase the variance of the gradient estimator by only $\frac{2(m+n+1)}{mn+1}$ relative to dense Gaussian ES, or $0.098\%$ for a $4096\times4096$ matrix. We then introduce LOO-ROLL, a leave-one-out estimator that preserves the finite-rank population field while replacing EGGROLL's two antithetic evaluations per direction by one. At equal evaluation cost, LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE in transformer blocks. At matched wall time across ten post-training settings and models up to 8B parameters, LOO-ROLL improves seven outcomes in individual paired tests, with no significant loss. On the GSM8K test set, accuracy increases from $38.1\%$ to $63.0\%$ at 0.6B and from $65.9\%$ to $80.0\%$ at 8B. Transformer measurements recover the predicted finite-rank variance, while the rank comparisons show no reproducible reward-based advantage for rank eight.

</details>


### [99] [Thompson Sampling for Non-Monotone Convex Ridge Bandits: Monotonicity Is Not Needed for Polynomial Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10981)
*Xuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thompson sampling for bandit convex optimization with non-monotone convex ridge losses has Bayesian regret $\tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt{n})$, showing monotonicity is not necessary but at a higher dimension dependence cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bakhtiari et al. (COLT 2025) proved Thompson sampling has $\tilde O(d^{5/2}\sqrt{n})$ Bayesian regret for convex monotone ridge losses and asked whether monotonicity is necessary. This paper investigates if similar regret bounds can be achieved for non-monotone convex links.

Method: The authors replace the single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy used in the monotone case with an $O(d^2)$ cardinality bound for "uninformative" configurations. They use a Boolean rounding argument: a 0-1 matrix within $1/(4r)$ in max-norm of a rank-$r$ matrix has rank at most $2r-1$. They construct $d(d+1)$ uninformative losses and provide a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer uniform over fixed measurable selections.

Result: For any prior on [0,1]-valued, 1-Lipschitz convex ridge losses with arbitrary convex (possibly non-monotone) links, exact-posterior Thompson sampling has Bayesian regret $O((d+1)^4\sqrt{dn}\log(e+nd\max\{1,\text{diam} K\})) = \tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt{n})$, showing monotonicity is not necessary but with higher dimension dependence.

Conclusion: The paper gives a qualitative negative answer to whether monotonicity is necessary for Thompson sampling in bandit convex optimization, proving regret bounds for non-monotone links with $\tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt{n})$ dependence. However, whether the $d^{5/2}$ dependence of the monotone case can be retained remains an open question.

Abstract: Bakhtiari, Lattimore and Szepesvári (COLT 2025) proved that Thompson sampling (TS) has Bayesian regret $\tilde O(d^{5/2}\sqrt n)$ for bandit convex optimisation with convex \emph{monotone} ridge losses $f(x)=\ell(\ip{x}θ)$, and asked whether monotonicity of the link is necessary. We give a qualitative negative answer. For every prior on $[0,1]$-valued, $1$-Lipschitz convex ridge losses with an arbitrary convex, possibly non-monotone, link, and for any fixed measurable selection of minimisers, exact-posterior TS has Bayesian regret $O\big((d+1)^4\sqrt{dn}\,\log(e+nd\max\{1,\diam K\})\big)=\tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt n)$. The monotone proof relies on a single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy; we show by an explicit twelve-point configuration that this dichotomy fails for non-monotone links, and replace it by an $O(d^2)$ cardinality bound for ``uninformative'' configurations. The bound uses a Boolean rounding argument: a $0$-$1$ matrix within $1/(4r)$ in max-norm of a rank-$r$ matrix has rank at most $2r-1$. We construct $d(d+1)$ uninformative losses, showing that the cardinality bound is tight up to constants in the large-diameter-to-gap regime, and give a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer that is uniform over fixed measurable selections. Whether the $d^{5/2}$ dependence of the monotone case can be retained remains open.

</details>


### [100] [Importance Weighting for Unlabeled-unlabeled Learning under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10994)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel importance weighting method for Unlabeled-unlabeled (UU) learning that adapts to distribution shifts between training and test data, handling various learning problems like PU learning and noisy label learning within a single framework without requiring assumptions about shift types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UU learning assumes identical class-conditional densities between training and test distributions, which rarely holds in practice due to distribution shifts. Current methods are limited by this unrealistic assumption and existing importance weighting methods cannot handle UU data effectively.

Method: Proposes a distribution shift adaptation method using importance weighting that minimizes test risk by weighting training data. The method works with UU data in both training and a few UU samples from test distribution, without requiring assumptions about shift types like covariate shift.

Result: The method enables principled importance weighting for UU data, allowing it to handle various learning problems (PU learning, noisy label learning, similarity-based learning) under distribution shift within a single framework. Experimental results demonstrate effectiveness on real-world datasets.

Conclusion: A unified framework for distribution shift adaptation in UU learning that overcomes limitations of existing methods by enabling principled importance weighting without restrictive assumptions, applicable to diverse learning problems while maintaining practical effectiveness.

Abstract: Unlabeled-unlabeled (UU) learning allows us to learn a binary classifier from two sets of unlabeled data with different class-priors. It is a general framework because it includes a wide variety of supervised learning such as positive-unlabeled (PU) learning, noisy label learning, and similarity-based learning. Existing UU learning assumes that the test and training distributions have the same class-conditional densities. However, this assumption rarely holds in practice due to distribution shifts.
