This paper proposes a distribution shift adaptation method for UU learning that uses UU data in the training distribution and a few UU data in the test distribution. The proposed method is based on the importance weighting, which minimizes the test risk by using training data with estimated importance weights. Although existing importance weighting methods cannot handle UU data, we show that it can be done in a principled manner. Thanks to the generality of UU learning, our method can handle various learning problems such as PU and noisy label learning under distribution shift within a single framework while existing methods are usually tailored to a specific problem. Moreover, it does not require any assumption of the shift types such as covariate shift. We experimentally demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method with real-world datasets.

</details>


### [101] [Topological Necessities: Mechanism-Invariant Strategic Subgoals for Cross-Embodiment Goal-Conditioned Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11014)
*Hao Shi,Xi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "topological necessities" - certified gates that form unavoidable stages in goal-directed tasks, recoverable from offline trajectories and transferable across different robot embodiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing subgoal methods in goal-conditioned RL produce implicit subgoals tied to specific executors. The authors seek to identify objective, unavoidable stages that every successful executor must pass through, independent of the particular agent embodiment.

Method: Use topological analysis (homology in dimensions 0 and 1) over a transport-weighted carrier built from successful trajectories to identify "unskippable stages" (separating sets) and "route choices" (loops in free space). This yields an enumerable set of certified "topological necessities" gates that form a hierarchical structure.

Result: The topological gates transfer without retraining across different embodiments (from PointMaze to Ant and Humanoid), achieving 96.1 aggregate score on Humanoid, +36.0 improvement over baselines. Saturation performance on PointMaze (100±0), strong improvements on AntMaze (+22.9) and Kitchen environments (+15.8/+12.6).

Conclusion: Topological necessities provide executor-independent subgoals that transfer across embodiments, offering a principled way to identify objective bottlenecks in task structure that work across different agent morphologies without retraining.

Abstract: Long-horizon goal-conditioned reinforcement learning delegates control to a high-level module that proposes subgoals, but existing subgoals are implicit byproducts of value functions or latent actions, tied to the executor that produced them. We study a different object: a route-conditioned order of unavoidable stages that every successful executor must traverse, recoverable from offline trajectories and belonging to none of them. Its defining properties are topological: an unskippable stage is a separating set that every admissible path must cross, and a loop in free space forces a route choice. We read the two by homology in dimensions 0 and 1 over a transport-weighted carrier built from successful trajectories, yielding an enumerable gate set with shell-level certificates; the certified gates are what we call topological necessities. Certified gates enter the decision loop as a recursive topological gate hierarchy. Under a fixed, isomorphic free space, the object survives executor replacement: gates frozen on PointMaze data transfer without retraining to Ant and Humanoid, attaining the highest Humanoid aggregate under a unified interface (96.1), with +36.0 over a map-privileged reference on the multi-route task (p=1.4e-5); the planner saturates PointMaze (100+/-0) and matches or exceeds the strongest baselines on AntMaze (giant +22.9) and Kitchen (+15.8/+12.6).

</details>


### [102] [T1: Terminal Agent Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11042)
*Junyao Yang,Yucheng Shi,Zhongzhi Li,Ruhan Wang,Zongxia Li,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: T1 is a 122B parameter Mixture-of-Experts model trained with reinforcement learning to perform long-horizon terminal tasks in a real shell environment, achieving state-of-the-art results on terminal benchmarks while preventing benchmark overfitting through out-of-distribution training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent systems are increasingly tackling long-horizon tasks like coding and scientific discovery, with terminal tasks being particularly important but challenging due to their complexity and need for multiple tool-call interactions (300+ turns per task).

Method: Three key innovations: 1) Aggressive warm-starting with dense process rewards based on passing verifiers, 2) Stable optimization via TITO construction (training on exact sampled tokens with drift repair) and rollout routing replay (recording MoE expert choices), 3) Fully out-of-distribution training using isolated seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from benchmarks.

Result: T1 reduces training-to-inference log-probability difference from 0.021 to 0.013 with zero token drift. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, performance improves from 43.8% to 64.0% resolved. On Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, T1 reaches 27.9%, surpassing GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1 models.

Conclusion: The T1 model demonstrates that combining Mixture-of-Experts architecture with specialized reinforcement learning techniques for terminal environments enables superior performance on long-horizon tasks while ensuring genuine capability transfer rather than benchmark overfitting through careful out-of-distribution training design.

Abstract: Agent usage is shifting toward long-horizon tasks such as coding and scientific discovery, among which terminal tasks are especially important. We introduce T1, a Mixture-of-Experts model of 122B total trained with reinforcement learning, operating a real shell in a cloud sandbox for up to 300+ tool-call turns per task, rewarded by executing each task's own verifier. We provide a comprehensive recipe: First, an aggressively warm-started to stabilize actor-critic training, with a dense process reward scoring trajectories by the absolute number of passing verifiers. Second, stable optimization through TITO construction, training on the exact sampled token identifiers with drift repair at turn boundaries, and rollout routing replay, recording the sampler's per-token expert choices at every MoE layer and replaying them during training. Third, fully out-of-distribution training corpus: isolated seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from Terminal-Bench 2.1 ensures gains reflect genuine capability transfer over benchmark overfitting. Together, TITO and R3 cut the training-to-inference log-probability difference from 0.021 to 0.013, with exactly aligned zero token drift in the loss region. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, our post-train pipeline raises initial base model from 43.8% to T1 with 64.0% resolved. On Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, T1 reaches 27.9% and surpasses GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1.

</details>


### [103] [EMMI: Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence for Communication-Efficient MLLM Inference via Fused Representation Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11058)
*Motahare Mounesan,Irfan Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMMI is a system that enables efficient multimodal LLM inference at the edge by communicating compressed latent representations instead of raw sensor data, achieving 32x communication reduction with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal LLMs offer powerful reasoning across sensor modalities but are too computationally demanding for resource-constrained edge platforms. Current approaches either transmit raw sensor data (high communication overhead) or partition neural networks at intermediate layers, both of which are inefficient for edge deployment.

Method: EMMI performs modality-specific encoding, cross-modal representation fusion, and learned compression at the edge to create compact latent representations. These fixed-size representations are transmitted to server-side resources for high-capacity MLLM reasoning, rather than sending raw sensor observations or using layer partitioning.

Result: EMMI reduces communication payload by 32x while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy. It achieves up to 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions.

Conclusion: EMMI's representation-centric design provides communication-efficient edge MLLM inference with reduced overhead, improved privacy preservation, and a standardized interface between heterogeneous edge devices and server-side MLLMs.

Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language mod- els (MLLMs) have opened new opportunities for edge intelligence by enabling reasoning across heterogeneous sensor modalities, such as vision, text, and telemetry data. However, deploying these capabilities on resource-constrained edge platforms remains challenging due to the substantial computational, memory, and communication demands of modern MLLMs. Rather than transmitting raw sensor observations or partitioning neural networks at intermediate layers, Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence (EMMI) communicates a compact representation between edge devices and server resources, enabling communication-efficient edge MLLM inference. To achieve this, EMMI performs modality-specific encoding, cross-modal representation fusion, and learned compression at the edge, transmitting only a compact latent representation to server-side resources for high-capacity MLLM reasoning. This representation-centric design reduces communication overhead, preserves local data privacy, and provides a fixed-size interface between heterogeneous edge devices and server-side MLLMs. Evaluation on a representative multimodal benchmark demonstrates that EMMI can reduce the communication payload by 32x while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy, resulting in up to a 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions.

</details>


### [104] [The information geometry of large language models is shared, learned, and controllable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11063)
*Dario Picozzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that Fisher-Rao geometry of token probabilities provides a common mathematical framework for comparing language models, enabling behavior transfer, control interventions, and understanding of model learning dynamics across different architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While large language models exhibit similar behaviors, there's no clear understanding of their shared structure or how to modify specific behaviors without affecting others. The paper aims to establish a unified geometric framework to analyze and compare different models across architectures.

Method: The paper uses Fisher-Rao geometry on next-token probabilities to create a coordinate-invariant way to compare language models. It analyzes transformer, state-space, and recurrent models, studies geometry's relationship with predictive accuracy, scale, training, and conducts controlled experiments with language assignments, pretraining corpus statistics, and randomized evidence construction.

Result: Output geometries (Fisher-Rao on probabilities) show stronger agreement across architectures than activation geometries. Geometry predicts model spectrum/effective dimension, follows language laws, and enables pretraining corpus statistics to predict fact acquisition. The framework supports minimum-disturbance local interventions, reusable control transfers, and improves various tasks including steering, editing, and fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Fisher-Rao geometry provides a powerful, architecture-agnostic framework for understanding, comparing, and controlling language models. It reveals fundamental shared structures across architectures, enables precise interventions, and offers predictive insights into model learning and behavior.

Abstract: Large language models learn similar behaviours, yet it remains unclear what structure they share or how to change one behaviour without disturbing others. The Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities connects these questions: behaviour determines this geometry up to output-preserving symmetries, whereas activation geometry depends on coordinates. Across transformer, state-space and recurrent models, output geometries agree more strongly than activation geometries, and shared geometry supports semantic-category transfer. Agreement with human word choices increases with predictive accuracy, scale and training, and improves further after model-only calibration. Token probabilities and read-out geometry jointly predict the spectrum and its effective dimension. Controlled language assignments show that geometry follows the language law across architectures. Pretraining corpus statistics predict held-out fact acquisition without recalibration, while randomised experiments show that deeper evidence substantially delays acquisition across every tested architecture and evidence construction. Finally, the geometry prescribes minimum-disturbance local interventions, predicts their relative cost, and supports reusable control: updates learned on donor prompts transfer to unseen prompts while better preserving behaviour on reference prompts than Euclidean control. The same geometric correction improves steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning and fine-tuning.

</details>


### [105] [Beyond Solver Verdicts: Generative Reward Models for Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11085)
*Vikash Singh,Debargha Ganguly,Aman Goel,Ali Torkamani,Xiaoxue Han,Joseph Lilien,Ferhat Erata,Vipin Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new approach called Generative Verification (GenV) detects subtle encoding errors in neurosymbolic systems by using language models to verify reference-equivalence between formal translations, achieving high detection accuracy and downstream performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic systems rely on mathematical solvers that can't detect when formal translations maintain incorrect but verdict-preserving encodings (Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness). Existing verification methods fail at detecting these deceptive cases, creating a critical vulnerability in reasoning systems.

Method: Introduces Generative Verification (GenV), which distills an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a reference-free, continuous reference-equivalence score using language models' vocabulary space. The method uses decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders to extract spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training.

Result: GenV+HN achieves 0.961 AUROC in reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point downstream accuracy gain in agentic test-time compute allocation.

Conclusion: Generative Verification provides an effective solution to the fundamental vulnerability of verdict-preserving unfaithfulness in neurosymbolic systems, enabling robust verification that generalizes across different formalization approaches and significantly improves downstream performance.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic systems rely on mathematical solvers to guarantee reasoning correctness, yet solvers are fundamentally blind to whether a formal translation maintains strict reference-equivalence to a designated formalization. We formalize this vulnerability as Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness (VPU): a failure mode where an incorrect encoding executes successfully and matches the expected verdict. We theoretically prove that structural, verdict-only verification heuristics are mathematically bounded to chance-level detection on these deceptively valid traces. To resolve this, we introduce Generative Verification (GenV), which distills an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a reference-free, continuous reference-equivalence score by repurposing the language model's native vocabulary space. Mechanistic analysis via decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders shows this generative readout natively extracts precise spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training. Empirically, our oracle-mined verifier (GenV+HN) achieves 0.961 AUROC in reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point downstream accuracy gain in agentic test-time compute allocation.

</details>


### [106] [HERALD: High-Fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation for Heterophily-Aware Graph Condensation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11123)
*Sujan Chakraborty,Priyanka Saha,Saptarshi Bej*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERALD is a gradient-free graph condensation framework that adapts to graph heterophily through adaptive node scoring and feature selection, outperforming existing methods on heterophilic graphs while remaining competitive on homophilic ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph condensation methods assume adjacent nodes share the same label (homophily), which breaks down under heterophilic settings where adjacent nodes often have different labels. Current approaches using Weisfeiler-Lehman aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching don't handle heterophily well.

Method: HERALD adapts node scoring and feature selection based on measured graph heterophily. Features are selected via joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criteria that down-weight aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs. Nodes are scored by weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID), with weights controlled by a sigmoid function of heterophily ratio. Condensed subgraph is assembled through score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalised PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing.

Result: Experiments on eight benchmark datasets spanning homophilic and heterophilic settings show HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs while remaining competitive on homophilic ones across four GNN architectures.

Conclusion: HERALD provides an effective gradient-free graph condensation framework that handles heterophilic graphs better than existing methods, demonstrating the importance of adapting condensation techniques to graph heterophily characteristics.

Abstract: Graph condensation aims to produce a small surrogate graph that preserves the downstream node-classification performance of a much larger original graph. Existing methods rely on Weisfeiler-Lehman neighbourhood aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching, both of which assume that adjacent nodes share the same label, an assumption that breaks down under heterophily. We propose HERALD (High-fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation), a gradient-free graph condensation framework that adapts the node scoring and feature selection in the condensation pipeline to the graph's measured heterophily. HERALD selects features via a joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criterion that down-weights aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs, and scores nodes by a weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID), where the weights are driven by a smooth sigmoid function of the heterophily ratio. Nodes are then assembled into a condensed subgraph through score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalised PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing, all at an identical storage budget to BONSAI, enabling direct comparison. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets spanning homophilic and heterophilic settings show that HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs and remains competitive on homophilic ones across four GNN architectures.

</details>


### [107] [How Wrong Can a Good Predictor Be? Diverging Updates with Vanishing Predictive KL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11132)
*Qifu Wen,Shuaijun Liu,Zihan Zhou,Xi Zeng,Ningxin Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Even when approximate Bayesian updates differ significantly from exact updates (unbounded gap), they can still produce vanishingly small predictive error on stationary HMMs, showing that accurate prediction doesn't require exact posterior approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the relationship between approximation quality of Bayesian updates and predictive performance, challenging the assumption that large internal update gaps necessarily lead to poor predictive outcomes.

Method: Theoretical analysis of stationary symmetric Gaussian Hidden Markov Models (HMMs) with $K$ states, comparing exact Bayesian mixing with an explicit deterministic radial filter. Uses KL divergence analysis, confidence scaling, and examination of filter trajectories on belief coordinates.

Result: An unbounded gap between update maps coexists with vanishing predictive KL divergence for every fixed $K≥2$. The separation grows linearly with confidence scale while categorical KL vanishes. Expected terminal KL between filtered posteriors converges to zero along stationary trajectories.

Conclusion: Even unbounded internal update gaps don't guarantee predictive failure, isolating missing links between update discrepancies and predictive cost. The construction is limited to fixed K and doesn't provide universal criteria for when compression is harmless or when gaps incur task loss.

Abstract: Accurate posterior prediction need not require accurate approximation of Bayesian updates. We prove that an unbounded gap between the update maps can coexist with vanishing predictive KL for every fixed finite $K\ge2$ in a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM. Exact Bayesian mixing and an explicit deterministic radial filter act on the same $K-1$ belief coordinates. As $q\to0^+$, their separation in centered logits in the worst case grows at least linearly in the natural confidence scale $L_K(q)$, while their categorical $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathrm{exact}\|\mathrm{radial})$ vanishes at the same explicit witness. Along stationary HMM trajectories, the expected terminal KL between filtered posteriors also converges to zero at $H(q)=\lceil-\log(q)/c\rceil+1$. Typical blocks without switches drive both filters into a common confidence cone, where softmax curvature suppresses their disagreement; a single Gaussian maximal event controls adaptive noise. A sweep with equally spaced Gaussians over $K\in\{2,4,8\}$ illustrates the opposing trends, and binary controls at long horizons compare saturating and nonsaturating recurrences. The result isolates two missing links between internal update gaps and predictive cost: the contribution of separating states to expected loss and decoder sensitivity. Thus even an unbounded internal update gap does not by itself certify predictive failure. The construction is fixed in $K$ and does not provide a universal criterion for when compression is harmless or characterize when internal gaps must incur task loss.

</details>


### [108] [Phase-Decoupled, Model-Calibrated Power Control for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11133)
*Jae Gon Kim,Donghoon Yoo,Hanyul Ryu,Sungho Ha,Juyeon Lee,Soojung Ryu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated power management controller for disaggregated LLM serving that outperforms NVIDIA's Max-Q profile by achieving better energy efficiency with lower latency costs, especially for Mixture-of-Experts models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current power optimization approaches like NVIDIA's Max-Q profile show modest energy gains (+8.6% tokens/J) but carry significant latency costs (+5.2% mean e2e latency) and don't account for differences between prefill and decode phases in disaggregated serving. The one-size-fits-all approach fails to optimize for specific model, quantization, engine, and hardware combinations.

Method: Proposes a phase-decoupled controller: prefill lane runs under SM-clock window with latency guarantee floor, decode lane under automatic power cap calibration placed just above throughput/latency cliff. Uses runtime SLO guard with latency-gated calibration to convert SLO headroom into energy savings safely.

Result: On 8x B200 node serving Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8): balanced mode delivers +20.4% tokens/J at +3.5% mean e2e latency vs Max-Q's +8.6% at +5.2%. On Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4): all operating modes meet ITL-p99 SLO while vendor profiles miss it. Three-day sustained run saves 32.3% of lane pair's electricity. Method works best for MoE models (dense models recover ~5x less benefit).

Conclusion: Optimal power setting is specific to deployed configuration rather than GPU class; each serving lane warrants its own calibrated profile. The proposed phase-decoupled controller with automatic calibration provides Pareto improvements over existing solutions, achieving better energy efficiency with acceptable latency tradeoffs for disaggregated MoE model serving.

Abstract: Datacenter GPU power is the binding constraint on LLM serving capacity, and production serving has shifted to prefill/decode (PD) disaggregation. Deploying NVIDIA's Max-Q inference profile on a disaggregated B200 system, we found its realized gain modest (+8.6% tokens/J), model-dependent, and carrying a mean end-to-end latency cost (+5.2%) that throughput-only evaluation does not surface; the profile also applies one setting to prefill and decode GPUs that operate in opposite hardware regimes. We hypothesize that the optimal power setting is a property of the deployed (model, quantization, engine, hardware) combination rather than of the GPU class, that each lane warrants its own profile, and that converting SLO headroom into energy safely requires latency-gated calibration under a runtime SLO guard rather than a fixed recipe. We present a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller: the prefill lane runs under an SM-clock window whose floor is a latency guarantee by construction, and the decode lane under a power cap placed by automatic calibration just above a measured throughput/latency cliff. Because a disaggregated decode lane draws flat, memory-bound power, the cap binds continuously, the reactive-overshoot weakness that led POLCA to reject capping is absent, and the GPU's own power manager retains throughput under the cap. On an 8x B200 node serving Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8) under agentic load, our balanced mode delivers +20.4% tokens/J at +3.5% mean e2e versus +8.6% at +5.2% for Max-Q, a Pareto improvement on both axes. On Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4) every operating mode meets the ITL-p99 SLO in every repetition; both vendor profiles miss it. A decode-actuator A/B shows the calibrated cap beats static clock locks, and a three-day sustained run saves 32.3% of a lane pair's electricity. Both models are MoE; a dense model recovers roughly 5x less, so we scope our claims to MoE serving.

</details>


### [109] [Bidirectional Multimodal Fusion of Sky Images and Time-Series for Solar Forecasting with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11135)
*Ken Chen,Maneesha Perera,Wei Wang,Sachith Seneviratne,Hansani Weeratunge,Saman Halgamuge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SolCloudLLM: A multimodal LLM-based framework that combines sky images and historical time-series data for short-term solar power and irradiance forecasting, achieving significant improvements over baselines especially under cloudy conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-term PV power and GHI forecasting is crucial for grid operations but challenging due to cloud-induced ramps. Existing methods either rely only on historical numerical data (missing cloud signals) or use ground-based sky images separately. LLMs show promise in time-series forecasting but remain predominantly unimodal. There's a need to effectively integrate sky imagery with LLMs for better forecasting, especially with limited site-specific data.

Method: Proposes SolCloudLLM, an LLM-based multimodal forecasting framework that: 1) aligns sky-image patches with time-series patches, 2) performs bidirectional multimodal fusion of their representations, 3) creates a unified representation that is mapped into LLM embedding space. This approach combines physical sky imagery signals with historical numerical data through multimodal fusion.

Result: SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms baseline methods in MSE across all forecasting horizons on SIRTA and SKIPP'D datasets, achieving up to 25.4% relative MSE reduction. Benefits are concentrated under cloudy conditions. Notably performs best in few-shot settings, while other deep learning baselines degrade substantially and are often outperformed by non-learning physical methods in data-scarce scenarios.

Conclusion: SolCloudLLM successfully addresses the challenge of multimodal solar forecasting by effectively integrating sky imagery with LLMs through bidirectional fusion. The framework demonstrates superior performance, particularly under challenging cloudy conditions and in data-scarce few-shot settings, making it valuable for practical solar forecasting applications where site-specific data is limited.

Abstract: Short-term photovoltaic (PV) power and global horizontal irradiance (GHI) forecasts are essential for effective dispatch, reserve scheduling, and grid operations. At these forecasting horizons, errors are predominantly driven by cloud induced ramps: relying solely on historical numerical data may struggle to anticipate an incoming cloud, making ground-based sky images a crucial complementary physical signal. Furthermore, forecast performance is highly sensitive to location and local observing conditions, creating a strong need for site-specific data that are often scarce. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated competitive performance and high data efficiency in time-series forecasting. Despite their success, existing LLM-based forecasting methods remain predominantly unimodal, relying primarily on historical numerical time-series data. Effectively incorporating sky imagery into an LLM-based forecasting framework remains under-explored and an open challenge. In this paper, we propose SolCloudLLM, an LLM-based multimodal forecasting framework. SolCloudLLM aligns sky-image patches with time-series patches and fuses their corresponding representations through bidirectional multimodal fusion, yielding a unified representation that is subsequently mapped into the embedding space of an LLM. Extensive experiments on the SIRTA and SKIPP'D datasets demonstrate that SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms the best baseline methods in MSE across all forecasting horizons, achieving a maximum relative MSE reduction of 25.4%. Stratified analysis further indicates that the benefits of multimodal fusion are concentrated primarily under cloudy conditions. Notably, SolCloudLLM achieves the best performance in nearly all few-shot settings, whereas other deep learning baselines experience substantial performance degradation and are frequently outperformed by the non-learning physical method.

</details>


### [110] [LILA: Calibration-Free Structured Pruning of Large Language Models via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163)
*Sankar Behera,Dhruv Singh,Anshika Agnihotri,Raj Kumar Choudhary,Satyadev Ahlawat,Yamuna Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LILA is a novel structured pruning method for LLMs that uses spectral analysis (Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance on singular value distributions) to score neuron importance without needing calibration data, gradient computation, or auxiliary networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current structured pruning methods for LLMs require calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks during pruning, which adds complexity and computational overhead. There's a need for more efficient, data-free pruning methods.

Method: LILA scores neuron importance by computing the Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance between empirical singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated FFN weight matrices. This provides a closed-form spectral rule that requires no training, calibration data, or auxiliary networks. The method also includes dynamic sparsity budget allocation via KS scores.

Result: Without fine-tuning, LILA outperforms PruneNet by 1.57 percentage points in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B at 25% sparsity, and beats WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0 pp across all sparsity levels. After one epoch of LoRA fine-tuning, it matches heavily calibrated SliceGPT within 0.48 pp margin. NTK analysis shows 22× reduction in functional distortion vs random pruning.

Conclusion: LILA provides an effective, calibration-free structured pruning method for LLMs that achieves competitive performance through spectral analysis of weight matrices. The approach offers theoretical grounding via NTK analysis and reveals architectural bottlenecks at high compression levels.

Abstract: Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) offers hardware-efficient compression, yet existing methods require calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) scores neuron importance via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between empirical singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated feed-forward network (FFN) weight matrix, providing a closed-form spectral rule requiring no training, calibration data, or auxiliary network. Without any fine-tuning, LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by 1.57~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B at 25\% sparsity, and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0~pp across all sparsity levels, while preserving the original architecture. After one epoch of LoRA recovery fine-tuning, LILA achieves highly competitive performance, matching the heavily calibrated SliceGPT baseline to within a 0.48~pp margin across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2, despite using zero calibration data. A Neural Tangent Kernel analysis confirms a 22$\times$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral importance criterion. Finally, extending LILA to dynamically allocate sparsity budgets via KS-scores yields state-of-the-art generative preservation at moderate compression, while uncovering fundamental single-layer architectural bottlenecks at higher compression regimes.

</details>


### [111] [When does a spectral prior help graph learning? Connectivity-loss estimation under road-network disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11166)
*Van-Truong Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs estimate algebraic connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion using residual correction to first-order Fiedler sensitivity, showing improved accuracy over analytical baselines for rapid road network disruption screening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid evaluation of many simultaneous road-link disruptions requires a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation and local approximation for efficient network screening.

Method: Use graph neural networks (GCN, GraphSAGE, MPNN) to learn bounded corrections to first-order Fiedler sensitivity for estimating algebraic connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion. Test on independent, clustered, and targeted failures with graph-disjoint splits and zero-shot transfer to real OpenStreetMap networks.

Result: Residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391, residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257. Second-order perturbation provides minimal improvement. Sparse scaling extends to 20,000 nodes, separating one-time spectral setup from amortized screening cost.

Conclusion: Spectral residual serves as a useful but domain-sensitive inductive bias for structural connectivity screening, with mixed transfer results across different network domains but overall practical benefits for rapid disruption assessment.

Abstract: Rapid evaluation of many simultaneous road-link disruptions requires a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation and local approximation. We estimate relative algebraic-connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion using graph neural networks (GNNs) that learn a bounded correction to a first-order Fiedler sensitivity. The study considers independent, spatially clustered, and edge-betweenness-targeted failures, with graph-disjoint synthetic splits and zero-shot transfer to 13 OpenStreetMap (OSM) areas in six countries. GCN, GraphSAGE, and edge-aware MPNN backbones are compared with analytical baselines. In expanded OSM tests, residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391 (95% hierarchical interval 0.0151-0.0662), while residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257 (0.0095-0.0446). Second-order perturbation improves first-order MAE by only 0.0028-0.0053. Correction slopes decrease under targeted transfer, indicating residual shrinkage around systematic prior error. Leave-one-country-out OSM-to-OSM transfer is mixed: residual GCN improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0622 (0.0169-0.1153) but worsens the spatial point estimate. Sparse scaling extends to 20,000 nodes and separates one-time spectral setup from amortized screening cost. These results characterize the spectral residual as a useful but domain-sensitive inductive bias for structural connectivity screening. Code, cached networks, and reproducibility artifacts are archived at doi:10.5281/zenodo.22307723.

</details>


### [112] [Semi-Tensor Product-Based Multi-Term Randomized T-SVD and Its Visual Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11168)
*Xingchen Xiao,Feng Zhang,Wenjin Qin,Jianjun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MRSTP-SVD, a novel multi-term randomized semi-tensor product SVD for efficient low-rank tensor approximation that overcomes dimension constraints and computational limitations of existing tensor decomposition methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard tensor SVD (T-SVD) based on t-product has dimension compatibility constraints. Existing semi-tensor product extensions have limited approximation accuracy with single-term formulations and high computational costs for large-scale tensor data.

Method: Introduces a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under t-product framework with arbitrary invertible transforms, develops multi-term semi-tensor product SVD (MSTP-SVD) with multiple orthogonal decomposition terms, and accelerates it with randomized projection and power iteration techniques (MRSTP-SVD).

Result: The proposed method breaks dimension matching requirements while preserving T-SVD closed-form properties, significantly improves low-rank approximation accuracy over single-term schemes, and achieves better computational efficiency through randomization.

Conclusion: MRSTP-SVD effectively balances reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency, demonstrating superior performance in image/video compression and completion tasks compared to existing tensor decomposition methods.

Abstract: Tensor singular value decomposition (T-SVD), which is built upon the tensor-tensor product (t-product), has emerged as a powerful tool for processing high-dimensional visual data such as color images and videos. However, the standard t-product imposes strict dimensional compatibility constraints. Although extensions based on the semi-tensor product (STP) relax this restriction, their single-term formulations still suffer from limited approximation accuracy. Moreover, these deterministic methods incur high computational costs when processing large-scale tensor data. To address these issues, this paper introduces a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under the t-product framework induced by arbitrary invertible linear transforms. The resulting tensor semi-tensor product breaks the rigid dimension matching requirement of the standard t-product, while retaining the closed-form property of T-SVD. Based on this construction, we develop a multi-term semi-tensor product singular value decomposition (MSTP-SVD), which integrates multiple orthogonal decomposition terms to significantly improve low-rank approximation accuracy compared with single-term schemes. To reduce the computational cost of multi-term modeling, we incorporate randomized projection and power iteration techniques into the MSTP-SVD framework, yielding an accelerated multi-term randomized semi-tensor product SVD (MRSTP-SVD) algorithm that achieves a balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency. Experiments on image and video compression and completion tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [113] [Hierarchical Clustering Can Jointly Satisfy Richness, Consistency, and Scale Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11173)
*Daichi Kuroda,Maximilien Dreveton,Matthias Grossglauser,Patrick Thiran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hierarchical clustering can satisfy Kleinberg's axioms that are impossible for flat clustering, with many admissible methods existing that form a partially ordered set with diverse but constrained structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clustering lacks universal definitions, and Kleinberg's Impossibility Theorem shows no flat clustering method can satisfy all three natural axioms: scale invariance, richness, and consistency. The paper investigates whether this impossibility persists for hierarchical clustering.

Method: The paper examines the hierarchical analog of Kleinberg's axioms and constructs several admissible hierarchical clustering methods that satisfy them, including methods based on well-separated clusters and non-binary versions of single linkage clustering.

Result: Hierarchical clustering can satisfy all three axioms simultaneously, with uncountably many admissible methods existing. These methods form a partial order under refinement relations, with no greatest element but uncountably many maximal elements. Despite diversity, all methods share common structural properties like containing well-separated cluster hierarchies.

Conclusion: The impossibility theorem for flat clustering does not extend to hierarchical clustering - hierarchical methods can satisfy Kleinberg's axioms. There exists substantial but constrained diversity among admissible hierarchical clustering methods, with all sharing certain structural backbones while exhibiting significant variability in their partial ordering.

Abstract: Despite its ubiquity, clustering lacks a universally accepted definition of what is a cluster. Kleinberg's Impossibility Theorem formalizes this difficulty by showing that no flat clustering method can simultaneously satisfy three natural axioms: scale invariance, richness, and consistency. In this paper, we ask whether this impossibility persists when the output is a hierarchy rather than a single partition. We show that, in contrast to the flat clustering setting, the hierarchical analog of these axioms are jointly satisfiable. In fact, there exist uncountably many hierarchical clustering methods satisfying these axioms, which we call admissible. We explicitly construct several admissible methods, including methods based on well-separated clusters and a non-binary version of single linkage. For certain pairs of admissible methods, the hierarchy produced by one always refines that produced by the other. This refinement relation defines a partial order on the class of admissible methods. This partially ordered set has no greatest element and contains uncountably many pairwise incompatible maximal elements, revealing substantial diversity among admissible methods. Nevertheless, this diversity is constrained: every admissible method contains a hierarchy of sufficiently well-separated clusters, and every finite collection of admissible methods shares such a nontrivial common backbone.

</details>


### [114] [Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES) under Time-Varying Loss Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11207)
*Rahul Vaze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends CONES to include time-varying convex loss functions, achieving simultaneous regret-movement tradeoffs via projected proximal algorithm with matching lower bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper extends the CONES framework which previously had a fixed objective function with evolving feasible sets. The motivation is to handle more realistic scenarios where both the loss functions and feasible regions change over time, creating a more general online convex optimization problem with nested constraints.

Method: Uses projected proximal algorithm to handle time-varying convex loss functions with nested feasible sets. Analyzes both convex and strongly convex loss functions, establishing theoretical guarantees for simultaneous regret and movement cost minimization.

Result: For convex loss functions: achieves O(T^{1-β}) regret and O(T^β) movement cost for any β∈[0,1), with matching lower bound Ω(T^{(1-β)/2}) movement cost for any weakly adaptive algorithm. For strongly convex loss functions: achieves O(1) regret and O(log T) movement cost, with matching Ω(log T) lower bound for any algorithm with sublinear anytime regret.

Conclusion: The paper successfully extends CONES to time-varying loss functions, providing optimal algorithms with tight tradeoffs between regret and movement cost for both convex and strongly convex settings, establishing fundamental performance limits for this general online optimization framework.

Abstract: Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES)} was introduced in \cite{CONESVaze} where the objective function \(f\) remains fixed but the feasible region evolves over time as a nested sequence \(S_1 \supseteq S_2 \supseteq \cdots \supseteq S_T\). The goal of an online algorithm is to simultaneously minimize the regret with respect to hindsight static optimal benchmark and the total movement cost $M_\cA(T)$ while ensuring feasibility at all times. CONES is an optimization-oriented generalization of the well-known \emph{nested convex body chasing} (NCBC). In this paper, we extend CONES to allow for loss functions $f_t'$s to also change over time. When all loss functions are convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm achieves $O(T^{1-β}), O(T^β)$ simultaneous regret and movement cost, respectively, for any $β\in [0,1)$, over a time horizon of $T$. We also show that any {\it weakly adaptive} online algorithm with $O(T^β)$ regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(T^{\frac{1-β}{2}}\right)$ for any $β\in [0,1)$. When all loss functions are strongly convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm simultaneously achieves $O(1)$ regret and a movement cost of $O(\log T)$. To complement this, we show that any online algorithm with sublinear {\it anytime} regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(\log T\right)$.

</details>


### [115] [REVA: Reusable Evidence View Aggregation for Context-Efficient RAG Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11209)
*Tuan Nguyen,Qiran Hu,Banruo Liu,Khoa D. Doan,Kok-Seng Wong,Fan Lai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REVA is a framework that mines historical LLM attention traces to create reusable, budget-aware document compressions for RAG, reducing latency and improving generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval-augmented generation faces efficiency challenges with longer contexts (increased latency, KV cache memory, token costs). Existing post-retrieval compressors often have unstable gains, operate independently per query, rely on auxiliary models, and introduce online overhead that can offset compression benefits.

Method: REVA aggregates historical query-document-model interactions into reusable evidence views by: 1) Mining target generator's historical attention traces into document-keyed score store, 2) Mapping token-level attention to readable word units, 3) Aggregating importance across repeated document accesses, 4) Rendering budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order while maintaining standard RAG interface.

Result: Across four benchmarks and modern LLMs: improves generation quality by 1.0-5.8 points over existing methods; reduces compression overhead by 5.3x to 15.6x; adds less than 40ms latency.

Conclusion: REVA demonstrates that data-mining historical attention patterns for reusable document compressions is more effective than query-specific compression, achieving better quality with lower overhead while maintaining standard RAG interfaces.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves knowledge-intensive large language model (LLM) applications by conditioning generation on retrieved documents, but longer contexts increase latency, key-value (KV) cache memory, and token cost. Post-retrieval compression can reduce this cost, yet existing compressors often operate independently for each query, rely on auxiliary models or rewriting, and introduce online overhead that can offset the benefit of shorter prompts. We revisit RAG compression from a data-mining perspective by aggregating historical query--document--model interactions into reusable evidence views. We first show that modern compressors have unstable gains over simple truncation and can add substantial inference-time latency. We then propose Reusable Evidence View Aggregation (REVA), a framework that mines the target generator's historical attention traces into a document-keyed, budget-agnostic score store. REVA maps token-level attention to readable word units, aggregates importance across repeated document accesses, and renders budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order and the standard RAG interface. Across four representative benchmarks and modern LLMs, REVA improves generation quality by 1.0--5.8 points over existing advances, while reducing compression overhead by a factor of 5.3 to 15.6, adding less than 40 ms of latency.

</details>


### [116] [Legible Failures: Detecting and Repairing In-Context Binding Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11216)
*Manas Venkata Sai Ravulapalli,Samrath Singh Chadha,Abhinav M. Hari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language models can encode correct entity-obligation bindings in hidden states while outputting incorrect ones, and probes can detect and correct these errors through steering interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether language model errors stem from lack of information or failure to use available information, using entity-obligation binding as a test case.

Method: Analyzed 16 public checkpoints across three seeds on entity-obligation binding task. Trained linear probes on frozen hidden states using training fold, selected optimal layer on validation fold, tested on disjoint test fold. Used query-entity counterfactual to control for token presence and recency. Tested steering interventions by adjusting residual stream toward probe-decoded bindings.

Result: On trials where models were wrong, probe accuracy exceeded baseline by +0.196 (95% CI [+0.101, +0.296]). Probe-based failure detection improved over model confidence by +0.079 AUROC (95% CI [+0.036, +0.126]). Steering interventions raised accuracy by +0.168 (95% CI [+0.066, +0.280]) across all eight tested models.

Conclusion: Probes can detect when language models hold correct information but fail to use it, and this information can be leveraged through steering interventions to improve model performance, contrary to findings that probe-detected errors are resistant to correction.

Abstract: A wrong answer does not show whether the model lacked the needed information or held it and failed to use it. On an entity-obligation binding task, a language model can emit an incorrect prompt-supplied binding while a linear probe can recover the correct one from its frozen hidden state. We measure how often this occurs across 16 public checkpoints, each evaluated with three seeds. We fit a probe on a training fold, select its layer on a validation fold, and report results on a disjoint test fold. On the trials each model gets wrong, probe accuracy exceeds the strict present-obligation baseline, 1/K = 0.125, by +0.196 (95% CI [+0.101, +0.296], bootstrapped over models). A query-entity counterfactual rules out token presence and recency. A score built from the sign of probe-output disagreement improves failure detection over the model's own confidence by +0.079 AUROC (95% CI [+0.036, +0.126]). Raw probe confidence gives no measurable improvement over model confidence. Steering the residual stream toward the probe-decoded binding, with no gold label, raises accuracy on all eight models tested by a mean of +0.168 (95% CI [+0.066, +0.280]). Where recent studies report that probe-detected errors are resistant to interventions, we find that in-context binding is a setting in which probes are actionable.

</details>


### [117] [Polyhedral Geometry of Time-to-First-Spike Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11227)
*Manjot Singh,Guido Montúfar,Gitta Kutyniok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Spiking neural networks with time-to-first-spike encoding produce piecewise linear input-output mappings governed by causal spike timing constraints, creating richer input space partitions than conventional ReLU networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the expressivity of spiking neural networks as an asynchronous, event-driven computational framework complementary to conventional feedforward neural networks, particularly examining how spike timing constraints affect function approximation capabilities.

Method: Analyze the time-to-first-spike model with continuous piecewise linear input-output maps, formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal spike sequences, derive theoretical bounds on causal regions in shallow and multilayer feedforward spiking networks, and conduct experimental validation.

Result: Neuron firing times have maxout-like representations with exponentially many constrained affine pieces. Upper and lower bounds on causal region numbers are derived. Both theoretical and experimental results demonstrate spiking networks can generate richer input space partitions than ReLU networks.

Conclusion: Spiking neural networks with time-to-first-spike encoding offer superior expressivity for piecewise linear function approximation compared to conventional ReLU networks due to their rich causal partitioning of input space governed by spike timing constraints.

Abstract: We study the expressivity of spiking neural networks, which provide a natural framework for asynchronous, event-driven computation complementary to conventional feedforward neural networks. We consider the time-to-first-spike model in a setting for which the input-output map is continuous and piecewise linear, with affine pieces governed by causal feasibility constraints that determine which presynaptic spikes occur before a neuron fires. We first show that each neuron's firing time admits a maxout-like representation with exponentially many, highly constrained affine pieces. We then formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal sets and derive upper and lower bounds on the maximal number of causal regions in both shallow and multilayer feedforward spiking networks. Our theoretical and experimental results show that spiking networks can generate richer partitions of the input space than conventional feedforward ReLU networks.

</details>


### [118] [Solving Few-Shot Multiobjective Multitask Optimization via Iterative Sequential Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11228)
*Tingyang Wei,Haofeng Wu,Ananda Phan Iman,Zhao Wei,Jiao Liu,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IST method enables effective multitask optimization under tight evaluation budgets by modeling it as sequential transfer problems with likelihood-informed task prioritization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multitask optimization approaches require aligning elite solution distributions across tasks, which creates a bottleneck in few-shot optimization regimes where limited evaluation budgets prevent identification of these distributions needed for beneficial knowledge transfer, especially challenging in multiobjective problems.

Method: Introduces Iterative Sequential Transfer (IST) that models multitask optimization as a sequence of sequential transfer optimization problems, concentrating evaluations on a single target task per iteration with likelihood-informed task prioritization to identify the task most ready for knowledge integration.

Result: Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems verify the effectiveness of the proposed method under tight evaluation budgets, demonstrating it can circumvent the elite solution distribution alignment bottleneck.

Conclusion: IST provides a practical solution to the few-shot multitask optimization challenge by enabling efficient knowledge transfer without requiring full elite solution distribution alignment, making multitask optimization viable under tight evaluation constraints.

Abstract: Applying knowledge transfer across multiple optimization tasks, multitask optimization (MTO) emerges as a promising approach to solving synergistic optimization tasks simultaneously. However, the development of effective knowledge transfer mechanisms in MTO fundamentally relies on aligning elite solution distributions across tasks. This dependency creates a critical bottleneck in few-shot optimization regimes, as restricted evaluation budgets impede the identification of elite solution distributions required for beneficial transfer. This challenge is exacerbated in multiobjective multitask problems, where each optimizer must approximate a continuous Pareto manifold rather than a single optimal point. This paper introduces Iterative Sequential Transfer (IST) to circumvent this bottleneck. We model MTO as a sequence of sequential transfer optimization problems, concentrating evaluations on a single target per iteration. We propose a likelihood-informed task prioritization mechanism to maximize transfer utility by identifying the task most likely ready for knowledge integration. Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems verify the effectiveness of the proposed method under tight budgets.

</details>


### [119] [MUtE: A Dual Framework for Concept Erasure and Counterfactual Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11253)
*Antoine Saillenfest*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel concept erasure framework that connects theoretical optimality with practical implementation, enabling seamless navigation between concept removal and counterfactual generation for improved fairness and interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concept erasure from representations is useful for mitigating bias and interpreting model decisions, but there's a gap between theoretical optimal bounds and practical implementation. The authors aim to bridge this gap by deriving erasure functions that naturally induce counterfactual mappings.

Method: Revisits optimal bounds of concept erasure to derive a novel class of erasure functions that induce deterministic dual counterfactual mappings. Designs an implementation imposing translational bias on counterfactual trajectories, aligning with how concepts geometrically manifest in language models.

Result: Empirically demonstrates the framework's efficacy in improving downstream algorithmic fairness and generating counterfactual texts, showing practical benefits of the theoretical approach.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully bridges theoretical optimality with practical representation learning, enabling seamless navigation between concept erasure and counterfactual generation for enhanced fairness and interpretability in language models.

Abstract: Erasing concept-specific information from representations has been proven useful for mitigating bias or interpreting model decisions. The joint objective is to transform the original representations such that the target concept becomes unpredictable, while maximally preserving concept-unrelated information. In this work, we revisit the optimal bounds of concept erasure to derive a novel class of erasure functions that naturally induce a deterministic, dual counterfactual mapping. Bridging the gap between theoretical optimality and practical representation learning, we design an implementation that imposes a translational bias on counterfactual trajectories - a constraint that aligns with how many concepts geometrically manifest in modern language models. Our framework enables seamless navigation between concept erasure and counterfactual generation. We empirically demonstrate its efficacy in improving downstream algorithmic fairness and generating counterfactual texts.

</details>


### [120] [A Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model for Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11314)
*Xue Qiu,Jianli Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DF-LLM is a novel traffic flow prediction model that combines large language models with spatiotemporal feature modeling through dynamic fusion mechanisms to overcome limitations of traditional neural networks and LLMs in capturing spatial topology and spatiotemporal correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional neural networks have accuracy limitations due to singular feature modeling, while large language models (LLMs) fail to adequately capture spatial topological information and spatiotemporal correlations in traffic flow prediction tasks.

Method: Proposes Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model (DF-LLM) with three core components: 1) spatiotemporal embedding module for multi-scale feature representation, 2) spatiotemporal fusion module integrating spatial topology via graph convolution, and 3) LLM backbone with differentiated parameter adaptation strategy, context aggregation attention module, and residual connections.

Result: DF-LLM achieves better performance compared to baseline methods across all four datasets used in the experiments, demonstrating superior prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed DF-LLM model effectively addresses the limitations of both traditional neural networks and LLMs in traffic flow prediction by synergistically combining spatiotemporal feature representation with LLM capabilities through dynamic fusion mechanisms.

Abstract: Traffic flow prediction is a core supporting technology for intelligent transportation systems. It uses historical data to infer future traffic dynamics in specific areas, thereby helping to alleviate congestion and improve resource allocation efficiency. Traditional neural networks struggle to break through accuracy limits due to their reliance on singular feature modeling, while large language models (LLMs) suffer from insufficient capture of spatial topological information and mining spatiotemporal correlation. This study proposes a Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model (DF-LLM) for traffic flow prediction. The model incorporates three core components: spatiotemporal embedding module, spatiotemporal fusion module, and LLM backbone. The spatiotemporal embedding module enables synergistic representation of multi-scale spatiotemporal features. The spatiotemporal fusion module integrates spatial topology and dynamic dependencies via graph convolution. The LLM backbone adopts a differentiated parameter adaptation strategy to balance training efficiency and traffic data adaptability. Additionally, it introduces a context aggregation attention module to strengthens global dependencies. More importantly, the LLM backbone takes the residual connections to mitigate the gradient vanishing in deep networks. Experiments show that DF-LLM has achieved better performance by comparing the metrics on all the four datasets.

</details>


### [121] [Estimating Inconsistency Response Surfaces under Uncertainty in Cyber-Physical System Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11331)
*Johannes Mäkelburg,Tim Schwabe,Maribel Acosta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a response-surface modeling approach to efficiently learn, analyze, and repair inconsistency in Cyber-Physical Systems under uncertainty, reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) use multiple interconnected models where uncertainty (from sensor noise, model abstraction, etc.) changes admissible values of shared elements, potentially creating inconsistencies across models. Existing approaches only determine consistency for given uncertainty configurations but don't support systematic exploration of inconsistency across large uncertainty spaces.

Method: Reformulates inconsistency as an intervention-response modeling problem. Uses Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation to generate intervention-response datasets, then trains a surrogate model to predict inconsistency from propagated uncertainty geometry. Builds on learned response surfaces for sensitivity analysis (identifying dominant uncertainty drivers) and introduces a gradient-based consistency recourse method to find minimal uncertainty interventions that restore consistency.

Result: Experiments on 48 scenarios across 10 CPS domains show the surrogate model matches Monte Carlo estimates while reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds. This enables orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed computational budgets. The approach effectively identifies dominant uncertainty drivers and finds minimal interventions to restore consistency.

Conclusion: Inconsistency under uncertainty in CPS can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, providing a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

Abstract: Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) are commonly represented through multiple interconnected models. During development, CPS consistency requires that shared model elements remain compatible across these models. Uncertainty, for example, due to sensor noise or model abstraction, changes the admissible values of model elements and can introduce inconsistencies, i.e., situations in which models can no longer be jointly satisfied. While existing approaches can determine consistency for a given uncertainty configuration, they provide limited support for systematically exploring, analyzing, and explaining inconsistency across large uncertainty spaces. We address this challenge by reformulating inconsistency as an intervention response modeling problem. Using Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation, we generate intervention-response datasets and train a surrogate model that directly predicts inconsistency from the propagated uncertainty geometry. Experiments on 48 scenarios and 10 CPS domains show that the surrogate matches Monte Carlo estimates while reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds, enabling orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed computational budgets. Building on the learned response surfaces, we perform sensitivity analysis to identify dominant uncertainty drivers and introduce a gradient-based consistency recourse method to determine minimal uncertainty interventions that restore consistency. The results show that inconsistency under uncertainty can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, providing a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

</details>


### [122] [Reification as a Transferable Vocabulary: Zero-Shot Link Prediction with Vanilla GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11347)
*Camille Pradel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new approach to zero-shot knowledge graph link prediction that reifies graphs into a fixed meta-relation representation, enabling standard GNNs to match specialized foundation models without pretraining on multiple graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current knowledge graph foundation models like ULTRA require dedicated architectures with hard-coded transfer mechanisms for zero-shot link prediction. The authors want to move this transfer capability into the representation itself rather than the architecture.

Method: Reify knowledge graphs by converting every fact (triple) into a node, connecting it to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations. Relation types become anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. This allows standard GNNs (GAT, GINE, GraphSAGE, R-GCN) trained on a single knowledge graph to transfer zero-shot to new graphs.

Result: Standard GNNs trained on just one graph (4,245 triples) for 30 minutes match ULTRA's performance on 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. GAT matches ULTRA's results despite ULTRA being pretrained on three graphs. The approach also extends to relational databases, showing promising results on foreign-key target ranking without seeing database schemas or cell values.

Conclusion: Transfer capability for zero-shot knowledge graph link prediction can be effectively embedded into graph representations rather than model architectures. This allows simple, off-the-shelf GNNs to achieve foundation model-level performance with minimal training, suggesting that representation engineering may be more important than architectural complexity for this task.

Abstract: Knowledge graph foundation models such as ULTRA achieve zero-shot link prediction on unseen graphs through dedicated architectures that hard-code a transfer mechanism. In this work we move that mechanism out of the architecture and into the representation, by \emph{reifying} the input graph: every fact becomes a node, connected to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations, with relation types as anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. On this representation, five textbook GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum and with mean+max aggregation, GraphSAGE, R-GCN), each trained on a single knowledge graph of 4,245 triples for 30 minutes on one NVIDIA A100, transfer zero-shot to 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. The best of them, an off-the-shelf GAT, matches ULTRA, a dedicated foundation model pretrained on three graphs, across ULTRA's own evaluation suite. The same fixed vocabulary extends to relational databases, a row becoming an entity and a foreign-key column a relation type; a preliminary probe on two unseen databases, with no cell values, schema text or in-context labels, shows a model of this family pretrained on three knowledge graphs ranking foreign-key targets far above random-initialization and degree controls. We release the code, the checkpoints, and the evaluation pipeline for all 40 benchmarks.

</details>


### [123] [Local Robustness Quantification for Naive Bayes Classifiers and Generative Forests: a General Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11366)
*Adrián Detavernier,Jasper De Bock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Methods for calculating prediction robustness in generative classifiers (naive Bayes and generative forests) by quantifying how much the underlying probability distribution can be perturbed before predictions change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop methods for quantifying the robustness of predictions in generative classifiers, particularly for assessing prediction trustworthiness and providing robustness measures that can serve as indicators of reliability.

Method: Define robustness as the extent to which the distribution of a classifier can be perturbed without changing predictions. Use Probabilistic Graphical Models (PGMs) as underlying distribution, specifically naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests. Consider perturbations within general neighborhoods using epsilon-contamination, total variation distance, and chi-squared divergence balls.

Result: Tested methods on benchmark datasets and demonstrated that robustness values serve as effective indicators for prediction trustworthiness. Compared the approach with other trustworthiness indicators.

Conclusion: The proposed methods successfully quantify prediction robustness in generative classifiers, providing valuable indicators for assessing prediction trustworthiness that can complement other reliability measures.

Abstract: We provide methods for calculating the robustness of the predictions of two types of generative classifiers whose underlying distribution is a Probabilistic Graphical Model (PGM): naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests (a probabilistic extension of random forests). Following the paradigm of robustness quantification, we define the robustness of a prediction as the extent to which the distribution of the classifier can be perturbed without changing this prediction. We consider perturbations obtained by varying the local models of the PGMs within general neighborhoods and focus in particular on epsilon-contamination, total variation distance and chi-squared divergence balls. We test our methods on benchmark datasets, demonstrate that the robustness value of a prediction serves as an indicator for its trustworthiness and compare our approach with other such indicators.

</details>


### [124] [Prevalence Determines Precision:Silent Contamination in Detector-Defined Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11449)
*Jia Huang,Yankai Wan,Yangjun Ou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ML dataset precision depends on true-positive prevalence in candidate pools via Bayes' theorem, not just detector quality. Using one detector across three pools yields wildly different phantom rates, demonstrating how contamination creates detector-shaped second signals rather than noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many machine learning datasets are constructed by applying detectors or heuristics to candidate pools, where accepted items become labels. The paper investigates the fundamental issue that dataset precision is governed by the true-positive prevalence in each pool via Bayes' theorem, not solely by detector quality. The researchers aim to demonstrate how contamination in datasets creates systematic biases that are detector-inherited rather than random noise.

Method: The study uses a single detector over three different candidate pools while holding both a detector-defined event dataset and an independent official index that labels every detected item as either real or phantom. The researchers analyze phantom rates across pools (81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%) and examine transfer of precision estimates. They also decompose the detected response curve into true-event and phantom components using convex combination analysis.

Result: The paper reveals dramatically different phantom rates across pools using the same detector (81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%). Transferring precision from high-rate pools to low-rate pools creates a +422% error, while Bayes expression predicts all three within 3.3%. The detected response curve is an exact convex combination of true-event and phantom components, with phantoms outnumbering true events 473 to 308. A common normalization method produces divergent results (0.40 vs 0.10) on the same 335 events.

Conclusion: Dataset contamination creates detector-inherited second signals rather than additive noise, leading to systematic biases where contamination direction depends on the estimator. Different statistics on identical windows can show opposite effects (dilution vs inflation) due to denominator contamination. Common normalization methods can produce mathematically undefined expectations and misleading results compared to well-defined estimators.

Abstract: Many ML datasets are constructed by running a detector, heuristic, or model over candidate pools; accepted items become labels. Dataset precision is then governed by true-positive prevalence in each pool via Bayes, not solely by detector quality. Using one instrument and period, we hold a detector-defined event dataset plus an independent official index labeling every detected item as real or phantom. One detector, three pools yield phantom rates 81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%. Transferring precision from the two high-rate pools to the low-rate pool predicts 0.955 versus measured 0.183, a +422% error; the Bayes expression predicts all three within 3.3%. The detected response curve is an exact convex combination of a true-event and a phantom component (residual 1.1e-16), with phantoms outnumbering true events 473 to 308, so contamination is a second signal with detector-inherited shape, not additive noise. Contamination direction depends on the estimator: on identical windows one statistic is diluted and another inflated because its denominator is also contaminated. A common normalization turns the estimator into a mean of ratios whose expectation need not exist; on the same 335 events it returns 0.40 where the well-defined estimator returns 0.10.

</details>


### [125] [Combining Synthetic and Real Data for Low-Resource Historical OCR: A Manchu Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11495)
*Yan Hon Michael Chung,Hanlin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vision-language models and compact neural networks achieve high accuracy (95-96%) on historical Manchu OCR when combining synthetic and real training data, with ensemble voting reaching 98% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manchu is critically endangered but has extensive archival records from the Qing empire that are difficult to search and analyze at scale. Current OCR methods have a synthetic-to-real gap, achieving only 87.4% accuracy with synthetic-only training.

Method: Evaluated three pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) and a compact convolutional recurrent neural network (CRNN) using 60,000 synthetic and 20,306 real historical word images. Tested four training regimes: synthetic-only, real-only, joint synthetic-real, and sequential synthetic-to-real training with common checkpoint selection and archival evaluation protocols.

Result: Introducing real training images raised accuracy to 95.09-96.28% word accuracy vs 87.92% for synthetic-only. Synthetic supplementation significantly improved VLMs. Compact CRNN reached leading performance when real images were available. Complementary errors among strong models allowed voting to reach 98.27% accuracy without additional training.

Conclusion: Combining synthetic and real training data is crucial for low-resource OCR on historical Manchu manuscripts. Model scale alone doesn't determine accuracy - compact models can perform well. Ensemble methods and principled rule-based adjudication using historical dictionaries can further boost accuracy for archival preservation.

Abstract: Manchu, now critically endangered, was one of the principal languages of the Qing empire (1636-1912), and its extensive archival record is increasingly digitized but remains difficult to search and analyze at scale.
